Cambio di rotta. Oaktree ha preso una decisione importante, che ora dovrà essere resa nota ai calciatori in procinto di rinnovo con l'Inter e che dovrà essere "digerita", come scrive il Corriere dello Sport. Si legge:

"Il piano dell’Inter, dunque, è quello di inserire nei contratti una serie di bonus collegati a titoli e trofei, ma anche ai passaggi intermedi che garantiscono premi per il club. In questo modo, i giocatori, da un lato, possono vedere dilatare sensibilmente i propri emolumenti e, dall’altro, vengono maggiormente responsabilizzati. E la società, di conseguenza, riesce a cautelarsi anche in caso di annata negativa. Il concetto, evidentemente, deve essere digerito dai diretti interessati. E non è così automatico. Ma è un approccio che vale in particolare per i giocatori che vanno per, o hanno già superato, la trentina: ad esempio Calhanoglu".

"Vuoi guadagnare ancora tanto? Va bene, ti accontentiamo, però, solo a patto che l’Inter vinca. Nel caso del turco, comunque, si può già immaginare che ci saranno anche alcuni bonus legati al suo rendimento: nello specifico alle presenze (della sua fragilità nelle ultime stagioni non si può non tenere conto), agli assist e ai gol. Insomma, ogni giocatore può essere artefice del proprio destino, inteso come guadagno. L’Inter è agevolata in tale percorso dal fatto che, in questi anni, è stata la squadra più vincente in Italia e che la prospettiva è quella di continuare a lottare per conquistare titoli. Basterà? E’ chiaro che, pur impostando in questo modo le trattative per i rinnovi, nulla vieta che ci si possa venire incontro, trovando una sintesi per le rispettive esigenze. A breve, con tutti questi fronti aperti, si attendono novità".

John Stones fin qui non ha dato le garanzie (soprattutto fisiche) che cercava l'Inter e dunque, in questi giorni, si stanno moltiplicando le voci su un possibile intervento in difesa dei nerazzurri nel prossimo mercato di gennaio. Sul suo canale YouTube, Alfio Musmarra si è espresso così sull'argomento:

"Continuo ad avere dei dubbi su Stones. L'Inter sapeva che era imperfetto fisicamente, ma voleva un calciatore che alzasse il livello nei big match. Speriamo che da qui in avanti Stones possa fare quel percorso. Altrimenti, l'Inter dovrebbe forse andare a prendere un difensore dalle caratteristiche che adesso mancano, ovvero quel marcatore cattivo che si attacca alla maglia degli avversari e non lo lascia respirare e che, al contempo, alza i compagni. Quello che faceva un po' Francesco Acerbi. E' quello che manca a questa squadra. Non so se sia facile da trovare, i dirigenti dovranno essere bravi a setacciare il mercato. Non so se si possa prendere a gennaio, dipende anche dalle condizioni di Stones".

"L'Inter dovrebbe fare una pescata alla Balerdi, a meno che non si tenti di fare una genialata. Io un nome ce l'avrei: tante volte si cercano nomi esotici, all'inizio per esempio nessuno pensava Acerbi potesse fare quelle cose. Ora, non dico che sia a quel livello lì, ma Baschirotto è italiano, è uno che dietro ha sempre fatto bene, è in una situazione particolare: ma da gennaio a giugno non potrebbe dare una mano eventualmente? E' un nome che mi è venuto in mente perché è forte fisicamente, esuberante, con qualità. Tante volte ci fossilizziamo sui nomi enormi, ma a volte li hai in casa e ti risolve tante situazioni. Lui potrebbe essere una soluzione intelligente, è uno dei più sottovalutati del nostro campionato".

La nuit de Kiev

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Après le carton de son livre Foot Sentimental paru il y a 3 ans aux éditions Le Cherche midi, Dominique Rocheteau a fait paraître il y a un mois dans la même maison Soif d'Idéal. L'Ange Vert revient dans ce bouquin sur le mythique quart de finale retour de Coupe d'Europe des Clubs Champions qui s'est déroulé le 17 mars 1976 dans le Chaudron. Extraits.

La veille, lors de la mise au vert au Novotel d'Andrézieux-Bouthéon, à une dizaine de kilomètres au nord de Saint-Étienne, je n'ai presque pas dormi. Je tournais dans mon lit, incapable de fermer l'œil. À 20 ans, je ne savais pas trop à quoi m'attendre. Je n'avais jamais disputé un match avec un tel enjeu, une qualification en demi-finale de Coupe d'Europe des clubs champions, devant notre public. Je me répétais que je devais dormir pour être en forme… mais cette insistance produisait l'effet inverse.

La journée s'est étirée lentement. Nous nous sommes retrouvés pour le petit-déjeuner, puis chacun a essayé de faire passer le temps comme il le pouvait. Certains jouaient aux cartes, d'autres lisaient ou discutaient dans le hall. Le déjeuner qui précédait les matchs ne changeait jamais : crudités, pièce de bœuf, purée ou riz, gâteau de riz en dessert. Comme un repère au milieu de l'attente. Puis la sieste…enfin, pour les autres. Moi, je n'ai jamais réussi à dormir. C'est long un jour comme celui-ci. Surtout quand tu ne dors pas. A l'extérieur, je paraissais calme, mais dans la chambre, je bouillonnais. 17 heures : collation, puis réunion avec Roby pour les dernières consignes avant – enfin ! - le départ.

Quand j'étais jeune, je me mettais toujours au fond du bus. Une habitude que j'avais, gamin. Plus on approchait Geoffroy Guichard, plus la ville se remplissait. Sur les trottoirs, aux carrefours, le long des routes, de plus en plus de supporters venus de toute la France.Des motards nous escortaient, c'était rare. Je l'avais déjà connu avec l'équipe de France, mais cela restait impressionnant. En arrivant au stade, vers 19 heures, j'ai ressenti quelque chose d'étrange. Un pressentiment. Impossible à expliquer. Cette soirée ne serait pas comme les autres.

A l'échauffement, sur le terrain annexe, à une centaine de mètres des vestiaires, on entendait le stade sans pour autant le voir. On ressentait cette communion qui unissait tout le peuple vert. Pour ce quart de finale retour de coupe d'Europe des clubs champions, le public attendait depuis des heures, en ébullition. Le Chaudron, plein à craquer, était en fusion et prêt à pousser derrière nous. Dès l'instant où nous sommes entrés sur la pelouse, j'ai eu l'impression que le public était avec nous, sur le terrain. Cette sensation, je ne l'ai jamais oubliée.

Déjà au match aller, 1500 supporters avaient fait le déplacement jusqu'à Simferopol, dans le froid et la pénombre, à près de 3000 kilomètres de Saint-Etienne. Ce mercredi 17 mars 1976, pour la réception du Dynamo Kiev, ils étaient près de 40 000, tous là pour vivre ce moment. Dans le vestiaire, de part et d'autre de notre gardien Ivan Kurkovic, personne ne parlait. On n'entendait que les crampons sur le sol. C'était saisissant, cette concentration. J'avais peur de ne pas être à la hauteur de l'attente de tous ces gens. On savait ce qu'on avait à faire. Revenir à 2-0 pour commencer, face à ce qui se faisait de mieux en Europe (nous avions perdu 2-0 à l'aller). Ensuite, tout deviendrait possible. Nous y croyions, le public aussi.

Christian Lopez tacle, Hervé Revelli marque. Le stade explose, comme si le couvercle d'une marmite venait de sauter. 1-0. Puis Jean-Michel Larqué, notre capitaine, efface notre retard d'un coup franc magnifique. 2-0. Il faut tenir, continuer d'attaquer. Prolongations... Mon mollet, touché 5 jours plus tôt à Nice, me fait de plus en plus mal. Je n'en peux plus. Je marche sur les talons, j'essaie de trottiner. Quand je fais signe au banc, à Roby, tout le monde détourne le regard, comme s'ils ne m'avaient jamais vu. Je n'arrive plus à courir, j'ai l'impression de ne plus servir à rien. Et puis, ironie du sort ou intuition de Roby, ce sont les deux entrants, Jacques Santini et Patrick Rivelli, qui déclenchent l'action qui amène au troisième but.

Juste avant de centrer, Patrick lance un regard dans ma direction, un dixième de seconde à peine. Tout le monde avance, je bloque ma course - j'avançais toujours sur les talons, incapable de courir normalement - pour me défaire du marquage de mon défenseur. Patrick fait un travail remarquable, et lorsque le ballon arrive, je n'ai presque plus de force, mais heureusement, encore un peu de lucidité pour tenter le geste juste. Tout s'enchaîne très vite : je vois le gardien s'engager, anticiper. Je soulève le ballon de l'intérieur du pied droit, au-dessus de Rudakov, premier poteau. À partir de là, tout m'emporte. Je pars en courant, un sprint de 50 mètres, tout droit vers les tribunes. Je ne sens plus la douleur. Je suis dans un état second. Je hurle, je saute, les bras levés. Hervé Revelli me rejoint. 3-0. Il reste encore 8 minutes. Ensemble, on tient, on résiste jusqu'au bout, jusqu'à l'épuisement. Et on l'a fait. Aujourd'hui encore, c'est de ce match qu'on me parle le plus. De ce but aussi.

La minute qui suit reste difficile à décrire. Nous étions chez nous, dans le Chaudron. La douleur était toujours là, mais je ne la sentais plus. Je reviens vers le centre du terrain en trottinant, entouré de photographes, comme porté par quelque chose qui me dépasse. Le lendemain, en découvrant les images, je ne me suis pas reconnu : la bouche en sang, le visage marqué par l'émotion et la fatigue. Ça m'a fait quelque chose. Après ce but, j'avais pensé à mon père. Puis tout s'était brouillé.

Mes parents étaient là, dans les tribunes. Je ne les ai pas vus, ni avant, ni après. C'était le premier match de Coupe d'Europe auquel ils avaient pu assister. Laisser le travail, les huîtres pendant deux jours, n'avait pas été simple ; ils avaient fait les 600 kilomètres en voiture. Mes cousins, des amis, le boucher, le coiffeur du village, le garagiste, le boulanger d’Etaules et d'autres copains de mon père étaient venus eux aussi. Après la rencontre, je n'ai pu voir personne. Ce n'était même pas la peine d'essayer. Trop de monde, trop de bruit, trop compliqué. Peut-être demain. Plus tard, ils m'ont raconté leur traversée de Saint-Etienne en fête pour rejoindre leur hôtel, l'Hôtel de France, au Bessat, un petit col dans la montagne.

Sur le terrain envahi, au milieu de la foule et des photographes, je ne me sentais pas à l'aise. J'ai préféré rentrer directement aux vestiaires. J'avais besoin de calme. L'émotion m'a rattrapé : je me suis mis à pleurer, je n'arrivais plus à retenir mes larmes. Même là, il y avait encore du monde, des journalistes, des dirigeants. Alors je me suis isolé comme pour me protéger. Je n'avais pas envie de parler. Je suis resté longtemps sur la table de massage, mon mollet était dans un sale état. L'hématome et la contracture que je traînais depuis plusieurs jours me lançaient dans toute la jambe. Jamais je n'aurais cru pouvoir jouer 120 minutes. Sans Gérard Forissier, notre kiné, je n'aurais pas tenu, c'est sûr. Il était minuit passé quand nous avons quitté le stade, la tête tournée vers la demi-finale.

Après le match, certains joueurs sont allés dîner pour prolonger la fête. Mes coéquipiers étaient mariés. J'étais le seul célibataire et parfois je ne me sentais pas vraiment à ma place. Et puis surtout, j'avais mal. Je n'avais qu'une envie, rentrer, me poser chez moi, aux Raymondes, sur les hauteurs de Saint-Étienne. L'effervescence était retombée. Comme nous étions au vert depuis quelques jours, le réfrigérateur était presque vide. J'ai fait chauffer une casserole de riz. Je ne savais pas cuisiner. J'ai mis de la musique comme d'habitude. Je me souviens de cet album de Neil Young, Harvest, de l'euphorie du Chaudron à Out of the Week-end. Hugues Boury, mon ami depuis le centre de formation et colocataire, était là avec moi. Avec Dominique Vézir, Jean-François Larios, Félix Lacuesta et d'autres, ils avaient regardé le match depuis le toit de Geoffroy Guichard avec des couvertures, parce qu'on pouvait y accéder depuis les chambres du centre, juste au-dessus de la tribune officielle. Nous avons fini la soirée sur le canapé à écouter de la musique sans rien changer à nos habitudes.

Inside this quantum computer, memory vibrates like a guitar string

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

A quantum computer might seem to have little in common with a guitar, but researchers at ETH Zurich have found a surprising connection. Physicist Yiwen Chu and her team have developed a system that uses microscopic mechanical vibrations to store and manipulate quantum information, much like the vibrating strings of a guitar produce musical notes.

Unlike the sounds produced by a musical instrument, these vibrations occur at frequencies far beyond human hearing. They take place within a tiny quantum chip, where they serve as a new form of computer memory.

By harnessing these vibrations, the researchers have created a way for quantum computers to process information efficiently while accessing a flexible working memory. "The interaction between the quantum processor and the quantum memory provides a crucial foundation with a view to establishing quantum computers as a powerful and reliable way to perform computations that are not feasible with conventional computers," says Yiwen Chu.

The breakthrough, described in the journal Science, introduces a different approach to quantum computer design. Instead of closely integrating information processing and storage, as many existing systems do, Chu's team has developed an architecture that gives these functions more distinct roles.

Quantum Memory Inspired by Conventional Computers

The researchers drew inspiration from the basic design of everyday digital computers. In a conventional machine, the central processing unit (CPU) carries out calculations while information is held separately in working memory, commonly called random access memory (RAM). The arrangement of these components plays an important role in determining how efficiently a computer operates.

Chu's quantum system follows a similar principle. A superconducting qubit acts as the processing and control unit, performing a role comparable to that of the central processing unit (CPU). Meanwhile, a separate quantum memory temporarily holds the information needed during calculations, allowing the processor to retrieve and modify it as necessary.

The major difference lies in how that information is stored.

"In our quantum working memory, however, information is not stored electromagnetically -- as is usually the case today -- but rather in the form of mechanical vibrations," explains Chu.

Quantum computers take advantage of unusual properties of quantum physics, including superposition, which allows quantum systems to exist in combinations of different states, and entanglement, which creates powerful connections between quantum states. These effects could eventually enable quantum machines to tackle certain extremely complicated calculations much faster than conventional computers, including some problems that are currently beyond their practical capabilities.

Why Vibrating Quantum Memory Could Be Better

One of the greatest challenges in quantum computing is maintaining precise control over quantum information. The states used for calculations are delicate, and even small disturbances can cause information to be lost. To perform calculations reliably, the processor and memory must interact closely enough to transfer and manipulate quantum states with high precision.

In the new system, microscopic structures called mechanical resonators hold information in particular patterns of vibration. When the superconducting qubit needs that information, it accesses the stored vibrational state, performs the required operation, and returns the modified information to memory.

Many existing quantum computers instead use electromagnetic memory alongside superconducting qubits. Researchers understand these technologies well, and they can be combined to provide extremely accurate control over quantum states, including reading, changing, and manipulating stored information.

However, electromagnetic memory systems have a significant disadvantage. They can occupy considerable space, making it difficult to expand experimental quantum devices into the larger, more capable computers that researchers and industries ultimately need.

Mechanical resonators offer several potential advantages. They are substantially smaller, allowing more compact designs. They can also support numerous different patterns of vibration, providing opportunities to store more information simultaneously than conventional electromagnetic memory.

Another important benefit is that mechanical vibrations can preserve quantum states for longer periods before the stored information begins to fade. Longer storage times could make it easier to carry out complicated calculations that require information to remain available throughout multiple processing steps.

The research published in Science provides the first experimental demonstration that mechanical resonators can work together with superconducting qubits to carry out quantum computations. The results establish that vibration-based memory is a feasible alternative to electromagnetic storage.

The achievement goes beyond simply storing information. Chu's team demonstrated that its architecture can handle both relatively straightforward calculations and more demanding quantum operations.

To evaluate the system, the researchers tested two important computational techniques: the quantum Fourier transform and period finding. Both are fundamental tools in quantum computing and require a computer to coordinate multiple quantum states with considerable precision.

"The Quantum Fourier Transform is a fundamental computational procedure required for many quantum algorithms. The period-finding algorithm we implemented served as a demonstration of how this procedure can be used," explains Igor Kladaric, doctoral student in Chu's team and co-author of the publication.

Successfully completing these tasks requires the system to store, control, and maintain coherent relationships among multiple quantum states at the same time. The experiments showed that the new architecture can meet these requirements, providing strong evidence that it can support genuine quantum computation rather than simply function as a memory device.

A Promising Step Toward Powerful Quantum Computers

One of the most significant findings is that the new system can, in principle, carry out all the fundamental operations needed for arbitrary quantum computations. This means the architecture has the essential capabilities required for a programmable, general-purpose quantum computer.

However, demonstrating those capabilities in an experimental chip is only the beginning. The next major challenge is scaling the technology to larger systems with greater processing power while preserving the precision and reliability demonstrated in the laboratory.

Chu and her colleagues are continuing to investigate how their approach can be developed further. Its long-term success will depend on whether mechanical quantum memory can remain effective as more components and increasingly complex computations are added.

Practical quantum computers powerful enough for widespread scientific and industrial applications are still some distance away. Nevertheless, by showing that tiny mechanical vibrations can serve as compact, capable quantum memory, the ETH Zurich team has demonstrated a promising new route toward making those machines a reality.

Materials provided by ETH Zurich. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Jensen Huang resigns: Nvidia shares tumble even further after scandal-ridden CEO replaced with interim boss

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Update: Thursday, 9:15 a.m. ET:

Jensen Huang has resigned as president and CEO of Nvidia, the company’s board of directors announced on Thursday.

The move caps a dramatic week for one of the most influential titans of tech, who had become embroiled in scandals surrounding two extramarital affairs, one with an alleged spy and another with a subordinate.

Huang’s resignation is effective immediately. He is also stepping down from the company’s board.

Colette Kress, who is currently Nvidia’s EVP and chief financial officer, has agreed to serve as Interim CEO until a permanent replacement can be found, Nvidia said. Kress will stay in her CFO role while serving as interim CEO.

Nvidia’s lack of a cohesive succession plan has caused much concern among analysts and investors alike.

Premarket shares of Nvidia Corp. (Nasdaq: NVDA) were already falling on Thursday morning as investors digested reports from the night before about a potential ouster of Huang.

Following the news of Huang’s immediate departure, the stock fell even further.

As of this writing, shares are down roughly 8% in early trading. The stock has fallen 15.62% over the last five days, since details of the scandal emerged.

This story is developing…

Original story appears below:

Shares of Nvidia Corporation (Nasdaq: NVDA) are once again sinking in premarket trading this morning after reports yesterday that the company’s board has convened to discuss whether to oust its embattled cofounder and CEO, Jensen Huang.

As of this writing, NVDA shares are down more than 7% in premarket trading, mainly due to uncertainty over Huang’s fate.

But several Wall Street analysts worry that Nvidia’s lack of a known succession plan could weigh on the stock’s price for a long time. Here’s what you need to know.

What’s happened?

This week started out bad for Nvidia, and yesterday things got worse.

On Sunday, CEO Jensen Huang admitted to an extramarital affair with a woman whom U.S. intelligence officials believe could be a Chinese spy. That raised severe national security concerns and led to a steep sell-off in NVDA stock.

However, at the time, Nvidia’s board released a statement saying it still had “confidence in Jensen and his leadership team.”

In other words: it was a scandal, but the board backed its CEO (and said it had no reason to believe its secrets or national security were compromised).

But things got worse yesterday.

That’s when The Wall Street Journal revealed that Nvidia’s board was meeting to determine whether to oust Huang. What changed? News broke of an alleged second affair that Huang had had, this time with a subordinate who works at Nvidia.

At this time, Huang’s fate remains unknown. But it’s precisely that uncertainty—and what comes next if he is ousted—that has Wall Street fearing for NVDA stock.

Nvidia has no publicly known succession plan

One of the greatest periods of anxiety and uncertainty for employees and investors is when a company changes leadership. 

If the old leader was good, can the new one measure up? Will the new leader change tack? Will the bottom line be impacted? Will the corporate environment change? What about employee morale and productivity?

Leadership changes often bring these kinds of questions, even in ideal situations, when a CEO change has been telegraphed long in advance (as was done with Apple’s CEO transition earlier this year).

But when a CEO change happens on short notice, especially when a scandal is driving it—and the company has no known succession plans in place—that really freaks investors out.

And that’s exactly why Nvidia stock is falling so much this morning.

The company has no known succession plans for who will take over when Huang leaves the CEO role. That only adds to the uncertainty now. It’s not only: Will he be fired? It’s: Who can successfully run one of the world’s most valuable and economically important companies if Huang is ousted?

Who can replace Huang as Nvidia CEO?

It should be noted that Nvidia may have a secret succession plan in place that the company has never publicly disclosed. But if they did, the board would likely have acknowledged the plan—or at least its existence —given the uncertainty of Huang’s fate.

Regardless of whether a plan is in place, if Huang were to be ousted, Nvidia has two options for replacing him: find a new CEO from within Nvidia, or look outside. 

It’s impossible to predict which external person the company would select as the new CEO, but someone with a deep understanding of AI or chip design would be likely. 

If Nvidia looks inside, there are some obvious candidates, based on their positions at the company:

  • Colette Kress: the company’s chief financial officer.
  • Ian Buck: VP of Hyperscale and High-Performance Computing
  • Michael Kagan: the company’s chief technology officer

CNBC reports that Citi analysts Atif Malik and Papa Sylla think Kress is a likely interim option while Nvidia searches for a permanent successor.

Morningstar says that if Nvidia recruits internally, Buck “strikes us as a logical successor to Jensen Huang” and “is well known to outside investors.”

But the fact of the matter is, for now, no one knows who would replace Huang.

Nvidia stock turns negative for the year

All this uncertainty is weighing on NVDA stock. As of this writing, Nvidia shares are down nearly 7.5% in premarket trading, bringing their five-day loss to over 13%—a staggering amount for a company that many see as the backbone of the AI economy.

Many investors fear worse times for NVDA ahead because of the scandal. The company is already being sued by shareholders, and a series of congressional investigations is likely.

Then the ever-present question remains: Who leads Nvidia if its visionary CEO is forced out?

“Right now, Nvidia doesn’t have [a Tim Cook to Steve Jobs-type] person in place, so it’s going to be a choice of keeping Jensen and being faced with . . . all kinds of interrogations, or they’re going to have to move on from him,” Questar Capital Partners’ Richard Reyle noted to CNBC, “and that’s just going to make the stock uninvestable.”

Uninvestable or not, the company’s share price has fallen more than 33% since its all-time high above $239 in early October, when Nvidia was valued at more than $5.7 trillion—the most valuable public company in history. 

Today, post-Huang scandal, the company’s valuation is down to less than $4.2 trillion.

(Disclosure: Morningstar is owned by Joe Mansueto, owner of Fast Company parent Mansueto Ventures.)

Cheddar cheese is being recalled in 12 states due to Listeria fears: Avoid these Family Farmstead Dairy products

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Listeria monocytogenes outbreaks continue to spark food recalls across the country.

This time it’s Family Farmstead Dairy, which has recalled all of its cheddar cheese products due to a potential listeria contamination, according to a notice posted by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA).

“This action is being taken as part of our ongoing commitment to continuous improvement and product excellence,” Family Farmstead Dairy stated in the recall. “The company remains committed to producing safe, high-quality dairy products and maintaining the trust of the communities it serves.”

The FDA announcement comes less than a month after the Worcester, New York, company recalled some of its raw cheddar products due to potential E. Coli O26:H11 and O45:H2 contamination. 

Here’s what you need to know about the latest Family Farmstead Dairy recall. 

What products are affected?

The recall is for two Family Farmstead brand cheddar cheeses. Here’s all the information you need to identify them:

Raw Mild Cheddar, 8oz, plastic wrapped

  • All lot codes dated October 16, 2027 or sooner

Raw Sharp Cheddar, 8oz, plastic wrapped

  • All lot codes dated October 16, 2027 or sooner

Where and when was the product sold?

Right now, we don’t know exactly when or in which stores the recalled cheddar cheeses were sold. All we know is which states they were distributed in:

  • Connecticut
  • Delaware
  • Massachusetts 
  • New Hampshire
  • New Jersey
  • New York
  • Pennsylvania
  • Rhode Island
  • Tennessee
  • Vermont
  • Virginia 
  • West Virginia

What should I do if I have this product? 

You can either return the impacted cheese to your point of purchase or contact Family Farmstead Dairy for assistance. The company is available at customerservice@familyfarmsteaddairy.com or (607) 397-4044 from 8 a.m. to 5 p.m. ET, Monday to Friday. 

What listeria symptoms should I look out for? 

Listeria is an organism that can cause sickness in people of all ages. Healthy individuals exposed to listeria will likely experience short-term symptoms including:

  • Abdominal pain
  • Diarrhea 
  • High fever
  • Severe headache
  • Stiffness

A listeria infection during pregnancy can lead to miscarriages or stillbirths.

Young people, elderly individuals, and anyone with a weakened immune system are at risk of more serious or even fatal results.

How advertising machines broke the attention economy

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

In 2016, TV advertising was in trouble. People were canceling their cable subscriptions by the millions, ratings were slipping, and advertisers were pouring billions of dollars into digital platforms that promised bigger audiences than TV at a much lower cost.

That April, Buzzfeed broadcast a live stream of two staffers in white coats and safety goggles wrapping rubber bands around a watermelon. The tension mounted as the rubber bands piled up, one at a time, hundreds and hundreds of them, squeezing the watermelon until it began to buckle. When it finally burst, sending pulp flying and its hosts shrieking, the live stream had been viewed more than 3 million times. CNBC called it the future of television.

Except it wasn’t. There was no way to tell how much of the audience actually watched the spectacle, or only watched it for a few seconds. The video may have simply run on autoplay as they scrolled past. But for advertisers, every three-second “view” counted as an impression, which was the industry’s basic unit of value.

Advertising had always been the business of attention. But attention is finite. There are only 24 hours in a day, and people can look at only so many things at once. But the algorithms that controlled digital advertising weren’t built to reflect that reality. They were given an incentive: maximize ad revenue. So they learned to produce and promote whatever generated the most impressions. An impression was counted each time an ad, or the content around it, appeared on a screen for a few seconds, whether or not anyone actually noticed it.

When the algorithms ran short of real attention to sell, they expanded into areas that required no attention at all: intercepting demand that already existed and claiming credit for purchases people would have made anyway. That set off a snowball effect. The supply of ad inventory exploded. The result was a flood of cheap, low-quality impressions—a subprime market for human attention.  As supply soared, prices fell for every seller of advertising. Never mind that TV commercials were graded differently. Scrolling past an exploding watermelon on your phone was treated the same way as watching every minute of a prime-time drama or a World Series game.

A vicious cycle

As television was forced to compete on digital’s terms, networks ran more minutes of advertising, which made the viewing experience worse and probably played a role in pushing even more viewers toward streaming and ad-free platforms. It was a vicious cycle. The less attention was worth, the more “impressions” the industry had to manufacture to make up the difference.

As traditional TV ads lost value, some networks experimented with cutting ad loads and rethinking the commercial break altogether. At the time, Jonathan was in charge of advertising at Fox Networks Group, and we experimented with a bunch of new formats: six-second spots, premium slots at the start and end of commercial breaks and even single-sponsor streams with minimal interruptions. Other networks pursued their own versions of the same idea, promising viewers fewer, better ads and offering advertisers more engaged audiences. These new formats provided a better value exchange for viewers’ attention. But they were a hard sell. Buyers asked why six seconds should cost more than 30. The real estate and retail industries valued placement and scarcity, but when it came to the advertising market, brands wanted volume. Real change required advertisers to let their agencies take risks and most wouldn’t do it.

A decade later, the stakes are even higher. There are still only 24 hours in a day, but artificial intelligence can now generate an unlimited supply of content. Feeds are filling up with AI-generated videos, and platforms are automating every step of advertising, from writing an ad to targeting the audience to placing the buy. There are now even ads inside AI assistants. ChatGPT says it’s on track to generate $1 billion in annual ad revenue, and more than 100,000 advertisers are running campaigns on the platform.

The risk is that an endless supply of content chasing a finite supply of attention will make each impression cheaper and less meaningful than ever. The subprime market for attention will continue to grow until advertising looks less like a market for attention and more like a toll on global commerce. AI will continue to optimize everything that is measurable, while the things most meaningful for people and businesses, such as culture, storytelling, trust and human connection—the hardest things for machines to measure—will be neglected.

But this new era of AI also creates a rare chance for a reset. AI can be built to serve people rather than keep them scrolling; helping us manage our time, filter information, make better decisions, and reclaim our attention. That would free us to spend it on what matters most: the stories we love, the experiences that bring us joy and, ideally, genuine connection with other people. That’s a future worth building.

Excerpted from Human Dot AI: How the Fight For Your Attention Became the World’s Biggest Business, by Joe Marchese and Jonathan Bing

Democrat Scott Colom wins in Mississippi to become the state’s first Black senator since Reconstruction

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Democratic nominee Scott Colom defeated Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith on Wednesday in Mississippi’s closely contested U.S. Senate race after a prolonged ballot counting process, giving the state its first Black U.S. senator since post-Civil War Reconstruction.

Colom’s narrow victory is a seismic shift in Mississippi, which has been controlled mostly by white conservatives for generations despite having the largest share of Black residents of any U.S. state. The outcome adds to a decisive midterm rebuke of President Donald Trump, whose party suffered defeats in several Republican-controlled states, and it will bolster some Democrats’ argument that their future runs through the American South.

“All I did was have the courage to imagine a night like this,” Colom told a crowd of hundreds who gathered at Tougaloo College for his victory party. “I had the courage to believe in Mississippi. I had the courage to understand that we can show the country that Mississippi matters.”

The Associated Press called the race in Colom’s favor after local officials spent several days processing absentee and provisional ballots, called affidavit ballots in Mississippi.

The last time Mississippi chose a new Democratic senator was 1947; the winner was a staunch segregationist. Colom, a 43-year-old local prosecutor, capitalized on the state’s large Black population, discontent with the economy and Hyde-Smith’s missteps as she sought a second full term.

“They actually deserve a senator that will listen to them, that actually cares about what they think, actually cares about what they’re going through,” Colom said in the campaign’s closing stretch.

Hyde-Smith, 67, called Colom late Wednesday afternoon to concede and released a concession speech online shortly afterward.

“While this is not the outcome we worked for or hoped for, I respect the decision of Mississippi voters,” Hyde-Smith said. “I want to congratulate Scott Colom and wish him well as he takes on the tremendous responsibility of representing our great state.”

Colom said during the campaign that he could represent all of Mississippi, a state where racial divisions have long defined its politics and society, and he repeated that pledge after his victory.

“My commitment to you, each and every one of you, is that first I’m going to treat your children like I want my children to be treated,” Colom said. “I’m going to treat your parents the way I want my parents to be treated. I’m gonna treat your future the way I want my future to be” and “work to make every Mississippian’s life better.”

National Democrats, activists and Mississippians celebrate

Party leaders hailed the senator-elect’s triumph, with nods to the South’s emerging status as a political battleground after other victories from North Carolina and South Carolina to Georgia and Texas.

“Scott ran the campaign of a lifetime and re-imagined what’s possible for Democrats in the South,” Democratic Senate leader Chuck Schumer of New York posted online.

Former President Bill Clinton, a native Arkansan who campaigned with Colom days before the election, addressed the senator-elect on social media, saying he won “because you believe in the promise of your state’s people. And they believed in you right back.”

Vera Finley was among those supporters.

“I am here to celebrate my parents and my grandparents. I am not one generation out of the cotton fields,” Finley said ahead of Colom’s victory speech. “Both of my parents picked cotton. And my great-grandmother was sold here as a slave from Virginia to here. So I am so excited to be on this side of history.”

Another Mississippian, NAACP President Derrick Johnson, issued a statement saying the state’s voters, most notably Black voters, “made history because they refused to be intimidated, discouraged, or counted out” in a state that has seen “some of the nation’s most difficult battles for voting rights.”

A race Washington once viewed as a long shot

The contest was a latecomer to the national spotlight, finally drawing attention and money months after Black senators, including Georgia’s Raphael Warnock and Maryland’s Angela Alsobrooks, first urged donors and party leaders to see it as winnable.

The son of a onetime Republican who worked for President Ronald Reagan, Colom produced one of the year’s most widely circulated ads. After Hyde-Smith seemed to brush off high beef prices by saying consumers could choose other proteins, Colom cut a 30-second spot depicting incredulous voters saying they’d “pick another senator instead.”

When Trump came to Corinth, Mississippi, in late October to rally with Hyde-Smith, Colom walked the line of Trump supporters as they waited to enter. He introduced himself and asked about their concerns.

Later, when Trump called Hyde-Smith to the stage, she said only nine words in six seconds before stepping away: “Is this not the best president in our lifetime?”

Colom’s appearance outside the rally tracked his strategy of criticizing Trump’s policies — especially the Iran war that drove up gas prices and tariffs that caused headaches for Mississippi farmers — without taking personal jabs at the president or people who voted for him. Instead, he consistently put the onus on Hyde-Smith, framing her as a nonentity in Washington — a critique that resonated in a state accustomed to long-serving, powerful senators.

There is some irony in Hyde-Smith losing to Colom. In 2022, President Joe Biden nominated Colom to be a U.S. District Court judge. Mississippi’s senior Republican Sen. Roger Wicker and former Gov. Haley Barbour, who is also a former national Republican chairman, backed the nomination.

Hyde-Smith, however, blocked Colom’s confirmation. Colom said his campaign was never about getting even with Hyde-Smith.

Colom called on Mississippi’s history

The senator-elect concluded his campaign with an ad that featured him at the Jackson home of Medgar Evers, the civil rights leader who, in 1963, was gunned down in his driveway by a white supremacist.

Colom told the story of Evers’ military service at Normandy in World War II, his voter registration efforts in Mississippi, his assassination and the three decades it took to win a conviction. “He was killed for helping Mississippians vote,” Colom said. “On Tuesday, use your vote and honor everyone who died for that right.”

At the lectern Wednesday night, Colom said it was appropriate that the race be called on Veterans Day, as he again paid tribute to Evers.

“He served his country, came home to Mississippi, and they wouldn’t let him vote,” Colom said. “But he had faith that Mississippi could make progress.”

Despite Republicans’ domination, the path was always there

Because of the Black population and its alignment with Democrats, Mississippi’s general elections have been closer for years than its Southern neighbors. Trump won by double digits in each of his three presidential contests, but in 2023, Republican Gov. Tate Reeves managed to win reelection by a margin of just 3 percentage points.

Colom and other Black party leaders and strategists insisted throughout 2026 that he could narrow the gap with strong Black turnout, Republican discontent and an uptick in Democrats’ usual share of the white vote in Mississippi.

Results showed strong turnout in the Mississippi Delta and its concentration of majority Black counties. But nearly all of the state moved leftward from the 2024 election, when Trump carried Mississippi by nearly 23 points.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to the Senate in 2018. She won a special election runoff in 2018 and a full term in 2020. Both times she defeated Democrat Mike Espy, a former U.S. agriculture secretary who’d also been Mississippi’s first Black congressman since Reconstruction.

During the extended vote count, Colom acknowledged the historic nature of his candidacy but said he cares more about what it means for others.

“My job is to do well enough as the next United States senator that the next time some young Black boy or girl has the work ethic, the vision and the values,” he said, “and they want to be United States senator, that nobody says because of anything I’ve done that they shouldn’t get a chance.”

—Bill Barrow and Sophie Bates, Associated Press

A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Exactly how would you test for this when it's an effect you're not going to see until you have installations measured in the tens of thousands?

The problem is that the "system" is much larger than the designers expected. The system is the complete set of buildings with UPS installed (thousands), interconnected through the power grid. For the designer, the system was just a single installation with no consideration of other installations.

The effects just aren't noticeable until you get to a large number of installations. No amount of local testing will find the issue. Just like in programming, no amount of unit tests will find all problems - there a

I'd use a random starting offset instead. So then the average is spread all round the 60 second cycle.

It would make be question the reliability of those UPS systems if they can't get basic stuff like that right. Randomizing anything where multiple units doing it at the same time could be an issue is pretty basic engineering.

Adding jitter may be obvious but it's wrong because it's overly complicated. You don't need a single instantiation to change from cycle to cycle, you just need different instantiations to be evenly distributed.

Least complicated might be a timer not synchronized to external time, but that may still be dependent on some other external conditions. Next best would be a offset(skew) set once, e.g. serial number mod 60.

His findings are about as relevant as the janitor's.

Apologies in advance, my petty ocd sensibilities were triggered by the headline.Perhaps the time is neigh

When I was working for a startup in the early 2000s, we had our own servers. We were growing, so were adding servers at a pretty good clip. After one particular expansion, all of a sudden, Saturday night at 3 am, we blew breaker. No one had a good explanation, but the sysadmins were not happy about needing to go to the office in the middle of the night to fix things up.

A week later, the same thing happened. I suggested that it was the RAID check kickoff that might be doing it. And, indeed, it was. A couple of dozen servers all seeking their drives at once was too much for the circuits they were on. Staggering the startup time fixed it.

Coherence can be good, like when you're rowing crew, or bad, like when you have dozens of drives all drawing max power. Or, from the summary, gazoober UPS systems doing a battery check.

Having corporate IT experience from administration to manufacture of banking systems, the system is supposed to be on a breaker that covers its maximum possible load, and such load be used in the calculations for sharing breakers. Wherever you worked was doing it wrong. The added amps from drive actuation should have been in the total breaker calculation along with maximum memory and CPU usage.

Simply staggering the array check time to slip by meant your circuit breakers were already on-edge without a safe

Maximum amperage draw for 1/10th of a second is not usually taken into account, breakers don't usually blow that fast because of a current spike. (Those numbers are usually not on the specification sheet either.)

The maximum load is calculated over a period of time, not a very short period of time. It is in this fuzzy gap that issues like this arrive.

Lessons weren't learned 180 years later either, for the Milllennium Suspension Bridge [wikipedia.org], which I happened to work next to at the time and watched the construction and failure unfold. [$] C++26 contract confusion

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The International Organization for Standardization (ISO) has blessed seven C++ language revisions since 1998. The language is mostly developed by ISO's Working Group 21, which considers proposed additions and amendments, integrates them into a document, and then presents that document to the main ISO body to be voted on as a standard. On October 23, for the first time ever, ISO declined to ratify a document presented by Working Group 21, leaving the future of the C++26 standard in doubt. The decision was driven by controversy around a proposed new "contracts" feature that would add pre- and post-conditions to function signatures.

Introducing Polo: a Wayland MATE compositor

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Kerem Söke has announced the 1.0 release of Polo, a Wayland compositor for the MATE Desktop Environment:

The idea is to keep MATE behaving as closely as possible to how it does on X11, rather than changing the desktop to fit a different compositor. [...]

Window management, metacity, workspaces, tiling, XWayland, clipboard and clipman support, multiple monitors, display scaling, hotplugging, screenshots, screen sharing, and session management are all working.

I've also added desktop effects: fade, zoom, zap, spin, squeemize, magic lamp, desktop cube, expo, and wobbly windows.

Söke is looking for people to test Polo, particularly on multi-monitor setups, and report back with problems.

What are some "No AI" apps/companies/resources you know of?

I'm not looking to start a discussion on what's good/bad or (un)worthy about AI, or who's for/against, just links and/or communities promoting this sentiment/position.

For example, https://www.rebeltechalliance.org would be one of those resources.

https://codeberg.org/ethical-foss/ai-free-foss-alternatives

It's such a shame that a list titled "AI-free FOSS Alternatives" ends up becoming the opposite of its stated goal, with the top level section being "Tainted Software" and occasional sporadic little notes listing alternatives.

I like the steadfast anti-AI people even though I'm not really one of them, and I want them to thrive and carve out a lasting space online. Putting more focus on the positives of the anti-AI vision and way less on the rejection and refusal stuff would probably help with that, and I think this is a really clear example where the negative side of the message has stolen the show unnecessarily. The "tainted" list is inside baseball stuff and belongs in the equivalent of a Wikipedia talk page on there.

Putting more focus on the positives of the anti-AI vision and way less on the rejection and refusal stuff would probably help with that,

As a general rule, I think that "focus on the positives" is a good rule of thumb.

As a blanket rule, it doesn't work. Sometimes it's really about the negatives. If you're trying to convince people, say, not to smoke or do heroin... really, "it's great not being addicted to things" is not as strong a message as "you'll get lung cancer if you smoke" or "this is what heroin addiction does to people". (Granted "just say no" was also terrible as a campaign, but for different reasons.)

I'm not saying that AI usage is equivalent to heroin usage, exactly, but I'm not sure that "the positives of the anti-AI vision" is as persuasive as the arguments against generative AI.

where the negative side of the message has stolen the show unnecessarily

Again, I don't really agree with this. There are some things that it makes sense to be against. This is especially true in the case of generative AI in my opinion because of the way it spreads and has basically overrun the industry in a couple of years. For some of us, it's the black mold or asbestos of software. "Gee, having a house without black mold is so awesome" isn't really quite the same as "here's what black mold is going to do to you if it infests your house".

Funny you mention smoking! Alan Carr's "Easy Way" book was a huge help to me in quitting, and one of its big central ideas is its focus on convincing you that life gets easier and better without smoking rather than browbeating you into increasing your willpower or scaring you with health statistics.

One nice example could be the text editor Helix, which I've used a ton lately. Its subreddit is almost rabid with anger about the slow pace of development progress but I see it as a selling point. The whole reason to use Helix instead of Vim or Neovim is that its design is distinctive due to the taste of the creators. Its reason to exist would be undermined if they adopted a feature factory mentality to maintenance just to appease the crowd, and it's great that they have the clarity of vision to avoid that trap and take the time they need to exercise their judgement about what goes into Helix. Plus it's a text editor and it's as good as finished anyway so what's the rush?

I think we could convince some of the more extreme AI maxis use cases exist where old fashioned coding by hand is optimal if stories like those get some reach. This would amount to a meaningful narrative shift in favour of the anti-AI people. But it can't happen in the case of Helix because the maintainers have coincidentally accepted a few PRs that contained some AI-generated stuff, and so the entire thing is shitcanned as "tainted software" by a page that ironically enough looks something Claude would churn out.

Alright, the AI version of that should be relatively easy to write:

Keeping and growing your coding skills seems like a useful thing to keep yourself employed in the long run. AI usage has been shown to lead to deskilling, and why would a business hire an expensive senior to push the buttons and make the AI do its thing when you can also get a cheap junior with the same skill set to do it?

Actually enjoying your work and understanding the code in your project can give more meaning to your work than rubber-stamping AI-generated PRs that come in by the firehose. And debugging code you actually understand when the shit hits the fan is easier than debugging someone else's code.

If you intend to stay in this business for longer, a deliberate pace can also keep you sane and prevent burn-out. The switching between longer bouts of code writing and code review can contribute to that. This beats quick task switching after spending a few minutes to set off a swarm of agents and getting back to the aforementioned firehose of PRs.

Regarding choosing software as a user: it's often been shown that people have trouble adjusting to change. If a project doesn't adopt AI, the changes that could be made from one release to the next are limited, as it's bottlenecked by humans doing the coding and reviewing. This leads to a more manageable pace of change that you can keep up with more easily. There's nothing so nice as software that's more or less "done", as you also point out in the case of Helix.

Empirically, humans haven’t found the bugs that have been present for a long time and that LLMs are finding now, so a project that takes the stance that the method of discovery makes knowledge of the bugs inadmissoble aren’t going to find the bugs independently, so they will eemain unfixed.

Rather than avoiding AI, why not opt for responsible usage of AI? LLMs speed things up, but it comes at a cost of losing correctness, which I do not think one can easily guarantee because of the way LLMs operate - statistically matching rather than logic/intuitive proofs which is what humans are good at. To get the best of both worlds, you want to use AI for smaller, more easily reviewed tasks, then use those (also w/ or w/out AI) to build a more complicated product.

Nixing a tool like LLM is IMHO a bad idea, if it can help us save time and increase quality of output, then so be it.

How do you actually self-host securely?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

I recognize that the title is rather vague, but I'm completely green when it comes to self-hosting, so I figured here would be a good place to ask for some pointers given the discussions I sometimes see happening in the comments. I tried researching first through DDG and Google, but (and I recognize this might be a skill issue) most things I found are either SEO slop or trying to sell/market some service.

For some more context and to give a concrete example as to what I'm interested in, I got my hands on a Raspberry Pi which I'd like to use to start tinkering with self-hosting. My main goal would be to host my (static) website on it, potentially adding more things in the future.

Some people I asked this question recommended solutions like Cloudflare Tunnels to make it secure, but using such a provider almost feels like it defeats the point of self-hosting, so I'm curious whether it's viable to do it using open source tooling, any gotchas one might encounter, and generally any thoughts you might have on the topic.

(Also, feel free to suggest a tag other than devops, I figured security doesn't quite apply since it's a question and not a write-up/resource regarding security.)

It sounds like what you may want is "nobody can read my packets but me!" If that's the case I would look at the chain of things.

These three are super intertwined, "self hosting" is choosing which of these you want to own.

You may end up using nginx or haproxy somewhere to get ports to line up. But you can also use iptables. Dealer's choice.

Tailscale and Cloudflare tunnels are really cool. They take a bit of the headache away, but you're right that they give up that bit of security (and the learning!).

I'm so psyched for you :) let me know how it goes!

I just drove across the Untied States with my backup MX. My phone provided a hotspot, a GL.inet did tethering and provided Internet, and my backup MX ran a tinc tunnel to give itself a public IP.

Since my backup MX runs off of 5 volts (USB powered) and has a battery backup, I ran it off of the cigarette lighter USB power adapter, but it could've just as easily been off my solar panel (which I run through the hatchback window :)

I always picture it baking in the sun on the cracked back dash of a 1996 Toyota Camry flying down the highway.

I have to admit that I smiled at this too.

I'm not sure if acme.sh is the best choice.

Also, for a lot of self-hosting setups (where you've got a server at home with services that you want to reach from other devices), I'd argue that Tailscale is overkill. Plain Wireguard is pleasantly simple to configure.

Sure, it has some niceties - it has NAT traversal, it's smart enough to figure out more efficient routing than the star topology you'd probably end up with in the simplest plain wg setup, it has fancy multiple user support - but do you actually need any of that?

The reason (the only reason!) I'm using Tailscale instead of WireGuard is that Tailscale has native apps all over the place (including critically for Apple TV and Google's streamer-du-jour), whereas WireGuard doesn't.[1]. Given I don't have to pay anything to use Tailscale, that makes it worth it for me for at least that specific use-case.

[1]: Well, there's a paid subscription app for the Apple TV, and I could jailbreak the Google thing, but there's a limit to how much effort I wanna put into just getting my Jellyfin files available.
