InfoQ Homepage Presentations Opening the Black Box: Critical Explainability for AI Agent Tool Selection with Kiji Inspector

Hannes Hapke shares how Dataiku’s open-source Kiji Inspector applies Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) directly to decision tokens to expose why agents pick specific tools. Moving past superficial chain-of-thought narratives, he discusses causal ablation, layer activation extraction via patched vLLM engines, and production monitoring workflows for software architects and senior engineering leaders.

Hannes Hapke leads 575 Lab, Dataiku's Open Source Office, focusing on responsible AI, privacy, and ML explainability. A Google Developer Expert for ML/AI and Google Developer Board member, he previously spent five years as Principal ML Engineer at Digits improving accountants' productivity. He is the co-author of four ML books, including Generative AI Design Patterns and ML Production Systems.

QCon AI is a practitioner-led event focused entirely on the engineering discipline required to scale these workloads safely. It provides direct access to the architectural playbooks and failure metrics that peer organizations use in production.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

Presented by: Kiran Gangadharappa - Engineering Advisor at Harness, and Scott Davis - Senior Engineering Manager at Harness

Hannes Hapke: Today we want to open up the black box of our lovely LLMs and agents and want to figure out what to do with them. My name is Hannes. I'm a machine learning engineer. I'm director of engineering at Dataiku, but I also have the great opportunity to run Dataiku's open-source lab, it's called 575 Lab. We work on open-source projects in the responsibility space. I've had the chance to co-author some machine learning publications, and I'm fascinated with machine learning systems, to put them into production, to work on open-source projects. I'm less the research person, I'm more like the person who brings machine learning models into production and building systems around it. I have the great chance to represent 575 Lab here. That is a wonderful organization within Dataiku where we can build open-source tools around responsible AI. Dataiku strongly believes that responsible AI has to be open, so anything that touches privacy or explainability, that is part of our lab.

Anything we share here and anything you will see, it's fully open source. We also contribute to other open-source projects like vLLM, but we have those two signature projects, one is the Kiji Inspector, that's what we're going to demo today, and one is the Kiji Privacy Proxy. If you want to protect your data from leaking to closed source models, that is something you can do, and also you can fine-tune it to your use cases.

For today, we want to focus on what we call the Kiji Inspector. It is a tool that is helping you to understand how agents "Think." The fundamental problem that we have with agents is that we don't know how they actually make their decisions. We have a number of tools out there. We have prompt engineering, behavioral testing, or probably the most sophisticated processes like chain of thought. None of them are actually providing you causality, they're often just giving you plausible explanations or plausible narratives of what the root cause could be. When we were asked to figure out how can we make sure that the model that is running an agent is picking the right tool, how do we know that it's thinking about the right context, we decided that we needed to look into the model.

The way we do this is by using sparse autoencoders. Before we get there, let me start explaining what an autoencoder is. This is a concept that has been around for a very long time, probably three decades by now. Autoencoders take a set of inputs, reduce this to a smaller latent space vector, and then reconstruct them through a decoder. The whole point of that autoencoder is that you want to minimize the error between the input and the reconstructed output. In an optimal system, there would be zero error, and the output here would be exactly as the input. Then you know that the representation of your vector z here in your latent space is most optimal. This has been used many times in various things like embeddings and vector searches and information compression, dimensionality reductions of various sorts of kinds. This is a really interesting way of forcing your model to reduce the amount of information to a smaller vector space.

The cool thing about this concept is that it's self-supervised. You don't need any labeling. You use the input as the output, that's your target, and then in between, that's what you would use in production. With sparse autoencoders, you would flip this concept around a little bit. Here in this case, we wouldn't create a bottleneck for the information, but we want to go wide here, with a little catch, and I'll explain the catch. Let's say you grab an activation from a model, or a hidden state, and you pass it through the sparse autoencoder, you can go wide in the information, and that means you force the model to pick certain features to represent the information. When you take a look at the activations inside of a model, there's something that we call polysemantic representation of the context, which means that one neuron that fires can have multiple meanings or topics it would fire on.

In our feature representation, if we want to understand the model, we need to separate them and put them apart from each other, and so that's why we're going wide in the feature representations here. Then again, we try to then have a decoder where we narrow that information piece again, and we try to reconstruct the activations that we used as an input. The catch here is that in this activation function, we are then forcing the model to only use a small set of features to be actually active, and the rest of them, roughly 95%, that's the ideal number, we want to force them to be zero. In that moment, we actually force the model during the training process to just be really selective on a handful of features, and then we can be absolutely certain that those features are representing the themes and the context of the model itself, what the model was thinking in this given layer at the processing of a certain token.

Then we can go one step further. Once you have those feature vectors you generated, those indices don't mean anything to us, to humans. What we can then do is, once we have a corpus, we can grab all the documents that fire highly on, let's say, dimension 3 and dimension 6, and then we can look at those documents and say, what do they have in common? It could easily be that they say like, the first one has something to do with Python, so then over the course of the entire corpus, we could say, those two features, they represent a programming language or an API vocabulary or things like this.

What we have tried to do in this project is combining a number of things and package them up so that everybody can use them in production. The first thing that we want to do is, sparse autoencoders have been around for a number of years now, and OpenAI published some research, and Eleuther published some research on this, but they focused on the entire LLM generation. What we changed here in our approach is we were only interested in the agentic decision-making. We focused on the decision token rather than the entire prompt, and then averaging those activations across the entire prompt. Then we put a bunch of concepts together. For example, Eleuther came out with this concept of token-level fuzzing, and so we added this to our framework so anybody who wants to use that framework and trains their own models, they can use that token testing. Then also we added causal validation testing as well so that you can make sure, is it actually causal or is it just correlation?

Then the other sweet thing, what we changed here in this project, what is novel, is that we're not just training on a number of prompts, but we created those pairs, training pairs. We have a given intent — I'll show you an example later — but for a given intent, you have something that we call the anchor that's the correct answer and the correct tool use, and then there's an adversarial reply, which is very similar, but very different. It's basically like look something up in an internal database versus look up something online. Two different use cases, very similar in the prompt response, but very different outcome in terms of the tool selection.

Looking at the training pipeline, let's say we have all these pairs generated, which I will show you how we do this. We then take our target model, and little caveat here, those have to be open-source models. We can't do this with OpenAI's models because we don't have access to their weights, but you can do this with any open-source model, and that's another novelty in our framework that we're not just focusing on Gemma models from Google or Nemotron models from NVIDIA. That tool, that framework here is working with various open-source models that are out there. You pick your subject model, that's the model you want to use the explainability for, and then you run all your prompts through the model itself, grab all the activations from the particular layers you want to watch. Then with those activations, you can train your sparse autoencoder as I just showed you with putting them in, widening them up, and then forcing the decoder again to reconstruct that activation.

Then once you have the model trained, you can do a contrastive analysis, and I'll show you this later, how we then figure out through those pairs of what's actually relevant in the context or not. Or you can go to a large language model and say, tell me what those documents have in common, and then you can generate those themes. This all happens in that package where we call the Kiji Inspector. You put in your corpus, you nominate those models, and out you get a feature definition of what those features mean when you run those LLMs and agent requests.

Earlier, I just mentioned you have to pick a certain layer. There's a little bit of an art what layer to pick rather than science. There's a little bit of an explanation here that if you're early in the stack, like maybe in the first third, the model hasn't fully conceptualized all the concepts of your prompt request. You're basically activating things that are highly relevant to the prompt itself. If you're further down the stack, there's a lot of intricacies of the model itself. Here in this particular model, we're looking at the Nemotron 3 from NVIDIA. That's an MoE model. That's because every different request goes through different routings, you have crazy errors. Learning something later on is very difficult. Also, the concepts you extract are so abstract in some cases that even for humans, it's impossible to understand what's going on. Then also for the models, it's difficult.

What we wanted to do is, for our particular experiments, we picked layer 20 because it is already down the pipe. It's like the model extracted some abstract concepts. It's not too far down the pipe that it would be too non-human understandable. In our open-source models, what we're publishing, we're publishing multiple layers. You can pick wherever you want to be in your model layer setup. Again, this is another representation of the training pipeline. Basically, you have your input. You calculate those representations. Then you do the reconstruction back to, in our case, it's 2688 dimensions. Then you calculate the loss. It's a very standard autoencoder model architecture. The key thing, and we show this in the following slide, we're using a particular activation, what we call the Jump Regularized Linear Unit. I will explain this here why this is in this particular case.

Then, how do we actually focus on the decision token? The way we do this is by forcing the agent to respond to a given prompt and says, I will use blank. Whatever tool, whatever the model then responds is expected to be the tool name, because we've provided the selection of tools in the prompt itself. Then in the response, we would see what tool the model has chosen. We do this a million times. We generated a training set with a million samples, basically 500,000 pairs, good and bad examples. The pairs come in later when we try to evaluate the model. During the training process itself, we use them raw, and we have those 1 million samples. Why are we doing this contrastive pair design again? Here's a little example. Let's say you have a given intent, resolve a password issue, and you can have the request, how do I reset my password?

That would trigger the tool knowledge base. You could also say, I tried to reset my password three times, and that could be a completely different tool. It's very similar in the response or in the request, but it's a very different tool outcome in this particular case. Later on, what you will see is we will then use those pairs and figure out which of the features we generated are actually relevant for those given tools. When we generate the features for both tools, the dimensions where the features are different have mostly contributed to the decision-making between knowledge base versus ticket lookup.

I don't want to go through the math here, but there's an interesting concept regarding the JumpReLU. As you know, Regularized Linear Units basically are, anything negative is zeroed out and anything positive is the input. It's like a linear function, but it's not differentiable because of that bend. Here in the JumpReLU, that's another derivative of the ReLU activation function. It's basically the output is the input, but there's a threshold. The interesting thing here is when we train the model of, figure out which activation is actually relevant and not relevant, we can then tune that threshold during the training process as the output activation. That's another tweak we can have to teach the model which feature is relevant for which given activation. When we look at some results per layer, then we see some really interesting side effects of early on, the error is low, but the model hasn't learned much.

Later on, the error is crazy high because this is logarithmic. Then we have too many, basically we learn something, but the error is exorbitant high. That's why we ended up in our practical example, we ended up somewhere in between at layer 20. Again, if we look at some stats here, like total, I think we had like 2,600-and-something input features, we widened this to 10,000 dimensions. Of those 10,000 dimensions, a little bit above 80% of those dimensions are alive. That means somewhere through the training, they have been nonzero. Then only less than 0.2% are completely dead, meaning those features have never been used through the entire training process. Then when we do the testing later on, we actually figured out that out of those 10,000 features, 668 are actually highly relevant to all the tool selections we wanted to investigate. This is for a given model for a given layer and a given dataset. If you retrain this, you might see different results.

Then somebody could say, why do we have to go through all this craziness of sparse autoencoders and training another machine learning model in all these crazy setups? As you can imagine, it's a lot of computation there. This is basically a couple comparisons. If you take the activations and then train a linear model on this, could we then figure out is this tool correct or incorrect? If you compare this and a binary classification here, we get to almost 80% accuracy. It's not bad, but it's also not perfect. Then if you do some just accuracy versus F1 score, that's a little bit lower. Then somebody could say, what happens if we just take those activations with those 2,000 dimensions and we just cluster them? Could we from the cluster then figure out which tool is the correct one? You're better off flipping a coin than actually clustering.

I think that was for us the interesting thing to see with the sparse autoencoders. I'll explain what fuzzing is. We can bridge the gap between interpretability and also having some causality testable features. I just mentioned token-level fuzzing. That was introduced by Eleuther sometime back when they looked into sparse autoencoders for entire prompts. What they did was they took a look at the prompt and then swapped out individual tokens, and then looked at how is the activation affected by swapping out those tokens and how is the end result being affected by that. That's the same concept we provide in our tool case here, in our framework that people can use when they want to evaluate their sparse autoencoders. Then when you look at the individual features and the quality of them, we can see that for the majority of the features they actually correlate really well to what we predicted the outcome to be. Only a small number of them have very poor influence on the overall decision-making. Again, from those 600 features we selected, it felt pretty good. This is heading in the right direction.

Then, let's take a look at causality and those other things. That's always the big question. We started this project where like, can we explain how an agent selected a tool? At some point, we needed to take a look at, can we actually prove causality? Little side note, I'm not a statistician, so I would love to have a better conversation about this with somebody who is more experienced with this. What we have done is looking at the activations. While the activation in this given layer in the model was produced, we then zeroed out the top 10 activations in that given vector. Then we looked at, is the tool selection changing or not? This was our setup. It's a little bit of like wiring and intercepting all the activations and those types of things. Don't worry, through the framework, we provide the other tools where you can replicate that.

The way this works is for our experiment setups here, where we looked at various tools, we were actually able to show that if you do the contrastive ablation, that worked extremely well for a number of tools. There were certain tools where we couldn't prove it, but for the majority of the tools, it actually looked very promising. Just a couple results here, the p-score is very low, so we were pretty happy about this. Through those testing, we were able to show that 23 of those contrast types actually worked. That worked better than randomly flipping. We felt that this is also, again, heading in the right direction. Like, yes, those particular activations, those 10 dimensions we blanked out or zeroed out actually contributed actively to the tool selection.

This was a lot of theory and a little bit of math and all those types of things, but you don't have to do this yourself, and that's why we have the Kiji Inspector, and that's why we open sourced that at Dataiku. What we're trying to provide here with a toolkit and the framework is like, we wanted to provide a model-agnostic framework that everybody else can use in the industry. Companies like Google have published similar frameworks, but they're always very tool specific. For example, Gemma Scope is the explainability tool for Gemma 2 or Gemma 3 or Gemma 4, or there's no Gemma 4 expanded model yet. There is no training pipeline. There's a lot of repos floating around with scripts and everything, but it's not like something you can download and fork and run something end-to-end on your site for your particular models and your particular use cases.

Then we wanted to provide a full production setup. We see a lot of research in those cases, but we hadn't seen a good production setup where people can run vLLMs and also get the activations back. Because in most setups like vLLM or SGLang, you get the response back but those inference tools don't give you the activations of a particular layer back. We provide a patch to vLLM that allows you to do that, and we also give you the containers so you can run this in production. Then as I said, everything is fully open source. We provide Docker containers, there's a PyPI package as you will see, and then all the models we have trained are on Hugging Face. This is only a subset, the list is way longer of what we're providing on Hugging Face. With our large training set of those 1 million data points or 1 million samples and the 500,000 pairs, we recently just trained Gemma 4.

Who knows if Google actually comes out with their sparse autoencoders. We wanted to beat them, and so we have the sparse autoencoders for the Gemma 4 and then for the latest Nemotron models, the Nemotron 3, we also provide the sparse autoencoders. Unfortunately, we don't have infinite compute resources as Google, that's why we have to pick certain layers to support them. If you have specific reasons, and you want a particular layer of a particular model, let us know, we can probably help you there as well.

How would the inference setup look like? If you run for example an agent and says, analyze my Q3 report, you would go through a large language model. Again, it has to be an open-source model, because you have to host that model so you can patch your vLLM version. We're hoping to get this into vLLM at some point so that hosting platforms like Baseten and others can provide those features as well. Then, you not just get the response back but you also get the activation vector for a given layer for your response. Then that goes into your sparse autoencoder. From your 2688 dimensions, you would then go up to 10,000 dimensions. Remember most of the dimensions are actually zero. Then you can take a look at the top features that are active in this large vector, and then you can take a look at those indices and figure out what are the themes you have determined during the training itself.

Then you can take those themes and put them back into a large language model as an explanation. Now somebody might ask, what's the difference now between doing all of this and chain of thought? The key difference is this information piece was directly derived from the activation during the model when that token was generated. It is not chain of thought where it first goes through some reasoning steps and then tries to execute. In chain of thought it could say like, here are two things I want to do, and then it executes 15 other things. There's no connection between those two steps. Here, there's the direct connection between the activation at a given layer. It goes into the sparse vector, and then we have the connection to the feature map we generated during the training.

Now the actually fun part starts. In Hugging Face, you can define what we call forward hooks. When the model is being executed, in Hugging Face the model is not compiled so you can easily hook into the execution, and say, give me the vector of a given layer for that given input. Then, we can grab the layer from here. When it's being executed, we can then actually ship it back to the response and send it to the user. I can show you another setup we have where you can also work with compiled models so you get a better performance. Then you run the inference, you retrieve the vector, and that is your activation. You're ready to go to use your sparse autoencoder. If you use one of the models we already support, you can then use our Kiji Inspector. We followed the Hugging Face setup. You can say like, from pre-trained, you mention the base model and under the hood we figure out like, what sparse autoencoder it needs to download.

We go to the correct repo, and then we use the correct model for the correct layer. Under the hood we convert this into the correct feature vector and then also do the lookup of the features to the themes. When you run SAE describe, that's how you get the description and not just a random vector. This is how it looks like, and I will show you this, again, but this is a little bit better readable. You see all the indices up here, they basically tell you what indices is the top five the ones that are firing. You see the number one it says unknown. What does it mean? It means like during the training, that feature in that feature vector was not of high importance and we haven't described that. Maybe we need more training data or maybe we need to tweak the training process. The interesting thing is that when we look at the second feature that was firing, it was actually something related to dishwasher gasket issues.

Seems relevant to the prompt we have. If it would fire on something completely unrelated here then there would be a good sign to go back and say like, maybe there's something going on in the model or maybe my prompt is incorrect or something else happening. Those are the things you can then see from the inspection and the data we provide through the package.

Let me show you here a little demo of how this would work and how you can actually use this in production. Again, this is the same code that I showed you earlier where we just stepped through. What are the particular use cases you can use for your applications and where you could use the Kiji Inspector. One thing is like you could figure out what an agent misbehavior would be. Anything that is like, has it picked the wrong tool, as I showed you earlier in the examples, that is a something. You can do it for audit compliance. Some companies looking at this and figuring out, it's like, if we have another track record, traces is one thing but explainability is another thing. When we do traces, we basically see what tool has been selected but we still can't explain why that tool was selected. Here we generate another set of logs and figuring out why the agent has particularly selected this tool that is shown in the traces. Then we can do validate of prompt engineering. If you're not quite sure, like you want to optimize your prompt for particular goals and you don't want to do this iterative loop, you can then also investigate like how is that tool responding to your given prompt based on the activations.

Then obviously monitoring production drift. If your data changes, let's say you test your agent with a set of prompts, you watch those activations, and then over time you can see if those activations change. That is a really good indication that the data that is going through that agent has changed. Maybe you should take a look at that as well.

Just to summarize this, what are the key takeaways? We have the chance to prove that sparse autoencoders can work to explain why the agent made a certain decision. We showed how the token-level fuzzing works, and as part of the package we can prove that tweaking those prompts lead to different activations. I also showed the causal evidence of the tool selection is affected by zeroing out certain activations in the model itself. Then we did some comparison with construction only baseline, meaning, because it's a decoder and there's a loss effect, if we take this out, then we can actually show that there's a causal effect in the sparse autoencoder.

I want to let you go with a couple limitations and also pointing out some future work. As I said, you do need the model itself. It doesn't work with closed source models. Hopefully at some point they give us similar tools on there, and that would be fantastic. The training is pretty compute intensive, especially if you want to create your own datasets. If you want to use the tool and you want to create your own training sets, come talk to us. We can share some more tricks and tips of how to run this effectively. As I mentioned earlier, it is a synthetic dataset. If anybody wants to use this and has some ideas of what is missing in the dataset and where should we go in terms of making it more real-world replicable, then let us know. We would love to collaborate on that as well.

There's a future work here. We want to support more open-source models and provide those models from the start through Hugging Face. Something that is on mind is currently Qwen. More multi-layer experiments. Currently everything is based on single layers. It could be really interesting if you take a layer from the first third and then a layer from the last third, and then combine those and see what actually happens. Then another interesting idea we had was like what happens if you do cross-model transfer. Basically, you train on a Gemma model. You take those vectors. You learn certain features. Is there something we can generalize then if we go to another model and so we see certain patterns. That is all future work. We haven't touched this yet. If anybody wants to collaborate on this let me know. Everything is fully open source. We live from contribution from others. This is not our project. It's a community project.

Participant 1: Is there anything that stops this from being applied to something by the training, of course, beyond tool calls. For example, if we were looking at safety alignment. The reason being that at least from the results of the causal inspection that you did, it seems to be possible to do some kind of steering after you train the model. Hannes Hapke: I think there's nothing that I would say that it's not possible. One of the things we're looking into is the security aspects of like, can we detect malicious attacks in those activations? Yes, I think that's possible. I think to me that's sort of the proof that if you just look at their pure activations you can't figure out what is going on. You do need the step through the sparse autoencoder. Yes, nothing is limited to just a tool call. For us, it was like a feasible project scope. The team is pretty small, so for us it felt feasible. Yes, totally.

Participant 2: Do you have any real-world examples where using your approach gave you a different understanding of why a tool was selected versus using chain of thought tells?

For example, this dishwasher repair thing, it picked water damage on an electrical equipment or something. It felt more precise than the actual chain of thought. There's a little caveat. The sparse autoencoder we trained for those examples, they were domain specific. Meaning that we were focusing on a very particular specific domain and that's what we built the training set for. There was a little bit of an unfair advantage. Also, it changes with model size. If you use a smaller model like the Gemma E4B, I think those interpretability tools have a benefit of it. Those were the two things where you could see the difference between the chain of thought versus the explainability tool.

InfoQ Homepage News Technical Debt is Social; it is the Price of Learning by Doing

Nov 12, 2026 3 min read

Technical debt is not necessarily a sign of poor engineering. It can be the price of learning through software in use. As AI accelerates code production beyond our capacity to understand code and context, technical debt increasingly becomes a sociotechnical challenge, Xin Yao noted in her talk "What If Your Biggest Technical Debt Is Social", at GOTO Copenhagen.

Yao quoted Ward Cunningham, who defined debt as "not-quite-right code". Debt sounds like a negative thing, but it can be a deliberate business strategy, she explained:

If we release the code now, well knowing it’s not perfect for the future yet, we’ll get some business feedback, we’ll get some user feedback, we’ll probably get paid for something, and that’s good as a business strategy.

Debt isn’t about poorly written code; it arises when we design software with incomplete knowledge. Hence, debt is the price of learning by doing, Yao argued. Releasing software allows us to test our assumptions against reality. Once we’ve got a learning experience, we have to put that experience back into our code and pay off the debt, she added. The debt becomes problematic when our software doesn’t catch up with our new learning.

Debt can be a good communication strategy toward management. It provides an economic argument for the cost of postponing architectural improvements. However, framing technical debt solely as something to eliminate misses its strategic value. The question is not whether to incur debt, but whether the learning it enables justifies the cost, and whether we actually repay it.

Yao referred to Kent Beck’s article "Earn and Learn," which explores features vs. futures. Features deliver value today, while the future is actually the optionality to adapt those features, Yao explained:

Every bit of code already coded is complexity and a constraint. Every feature removes some future. If you go on producing features, maybe using your LLMs, then at a certain point, all you can do is use your capacity to pay the interest, to fix the code, and you can no longer release features.

The challenge is to balance delivering features with preserving future options, Yao said. Sometimes, delaying a design commitment preserves options until we know more, and this includes postponing some upfront architecture decisions to the last responsible moment as well. As Beck argues, waiting is not laziness; waiting is holding an asset.

Technical debt can therefore be understood as learning made visible in software. We design based on what we know, observe software’s impact, modularity, and its architecture’s implications, and we update our understanding, Yao explained:

The software no longer fits what we now understand, and yesterday’s good understanding becomes today’s debt.

AI introduces a new challenge: software can now be produced faster than teams can develop a shared understanding of its behavior, design decisions and consequences, Yao argued.

Agentic coding makes debt repayment more complex; beyond addressing poorly written AI-generated code, teams must close the gap between what the software actually does and what they understand it to do, while ensuring the code keeps pace with their evolving domain and architectural understanding. Paying off debt is no longer just a technical task, but a sociotechnical one, Yao concluded.

InfoQ interviewed Xin Yao after her talk.

InfoQ: What does learning from technical debt look like, and what benefit does it bring?

Xin Yao: We design and release software based on our best current understanding, then test our assumptions against business and user reality. Paying off debt means incorporating that learning back into the software and closing the gap between what we knew when we released the first iteration and what we know now.

Each iteration creates new debt and new learning. Small batches and tight feedback loops help teams retain the memory of their initial design decisions and continuously repay the old debt with new learning.

InfoQ: What does technical debt look like now that developers are using artificial intelligence?

Yao: With agentic coding, software production can outpace human understanding. Code increasingly becomes a fossil record of agents’ decisions, while humans remain accountable for its consequences. Local productivity gains can create collective costs: more code, weaker ownership and growing gaps in shared understanding.

A study on How Developers Discuss the Burden of AI-Assisted Software Development describes this as "a tragedy of the commons". The danger is not merely more poorly written agentic code, but accelerated learning debt across the sociotechnical system.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

AI Raises the Stakes for Cyber Resilience

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

An organization's ability to quickly recover from a business disruption will be a competitive advantage in an era of AI-driven threats and business enablement.

Commvault SHIFT — Nashville, Tenn. — Enterprise data recovery practices must evolve from a last-resort response to a continuous operational discipline focused on minimizing business disruption and containing the blast radius of cyber incidents as quickly as possible.

That was the central message at Commvault's SHIFT 2026 conference this week, where security executives highlighted the growing importance of making operational resilience a business priority as AI-driven threats and increasingly autonomous business processes complicate the task of maintaining continuity.

"AI is changing the durable advantage you have built for your business, your brand, your supply chain, your data," said Sanjay Mirchandani, president and CEO of Commvault, in a keynote address at the event. "In the AI era, resilience will be your new competitive advantage."

Two developments are driving trend. On the threat side, AI is giving attackers the ability to automate and accelerate reconnaissance, exploit development, social engineering, and lateral movement. The trend has compressed the time organizations have to detect and contain an intrusion, Mirchandani and other executives at the event noted.

Related:AI Coding Agents Leak Thousands of Secrets to GitHub

At the same time, enterprises are increasingly embedding AI into business processes, workflows and decision-making, creating new dependencies on data, applications and autonomous systems.

The result is a more complex operating environment in which a disruption can have wider and faster consequences, making the ability to contain incidents, preserve trusted operations and recover without derailing business activity more important than ever.

Attackers these days are able move faster with a level of automation not available before, noted Rajeev Kottomtharayil, chief products officer at Commvault. Increasingly, recovery is becoming not just about speed but bringing back clean data. "If everything is under attack, how do we know what to trust? The real goal is to have a clean safe space to recover even if everything around you has been compromised," Kottomtharayil said.

Business innovation and enablement are also driving the need for greater operational resilience. Resilient operations give organizations the confidence to experiment with AI and put it to work across the business, while ensuring they can recover quickly when something goes wrong, said Pragnesh Dave, head of generative AI and ML sales at AWS. "AI is collapsing the amount of time it takes to move from an idea to an outcome," he said during a keynote session. As a result, the competitive advantage will come not simply from how quickly organizations can turn intelligence into action, but from how quickly they can do so while managing the risks and consequences when those actions sometimes prove wrong.

Related:People Are Getting Weird About AI

Dave pointed to the operations model at Amazon that emphasizes the notion of the "door swinging both ways," when teams try to innovate. It is an approach that has enabled teams at Amazon to code more than four times faster than before, he said.

"The question for everyone is not if something is going to break," he said. Rather it is about what the blast radius is and the time it takes from when something breaks to recovering to the prior trusted state. "If something goes wrong you need to be able to recover quickly. You should be able to walk back a decision. Think of your recovery time as your innovation project."

Continuous detection and continuous recovery are going to become a baseline requirement for organizations, argued Peter Diamandis, futurist, entrepreneur, and founder and chairman of the XPRIZE Foundation. Over the next few years, he foresees all organizations harness AI to migrate a significant workload to the "digital edge" with humans providing judgement, taste, and oversight. "AI gives us all access to capabilities that were once scare." The qualities that matter the most will be the speed at which an organization can innovate and that requires standing up capabilities to recover quickly when failures do occur, he said during a keynote address.

Related:AI Gave Your Incident Archive a Second Job

At the same time, recovery is not becoming a substitute for prevention, says Bill O'Connell, chief security officer at Commvault. Both are equally important. But "building the highest walls and the deepest moat" to keep threats out alone, is no longer enough. Organizations have to equally focus on building an ability to recover quickly to a trusted state regardless of the reason for a disruption and to thoroughly test that ability before it becomes necessary.

The key to keep in mind is that while AI has dramatically accelerated the pace of change it hasn’t altered the need for traditional, and long familiar IT governance and controls around their assets, identities, access, authentication and authentication, O'Connel says. "The IT maintenance and housekeeping elements still matter." When organizations have supercharged tools which they are not entirely sure how to govern, they need to make sure those fundamentals remain in place.

In enabling better resilience organization should also evaluate what impact third parties could have on their ability to quick restore operations, O'Connell says. For years, organizations have used questionnaires and external vendor risk-scoring services to try and assess third-part risk. In the AI era, security teams need to go a step further with their most critical suppliers, he adds. O'Connell suggested organizations have their technical architects work with their counterparts at third-party suppliers to understand how critical services are actually configured and what would happen if a provider experienced a breach or a disruption.

Attackers Say 'Hello' to a Different Scale of Cloud Attack

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Researchers dubbed an attack chain "Greeter" after discovering that attackers deliberately covered their trail by leaving "hello" messages in logs.

What began as an exploratory Shodan search for exposed cloud instances has led to the discovery of a broad-ranging AI infrastructure attack campaign that researchers have christened "Greeter." The campaign, which started life as an organized, multistage, systematic attack across cloud providers, reinforces why defenders need to shift their thinking about cloud security to include a layered model built around network access control, data protection, and identity security.

Era Security researchers kicked off the research when the Shodan search engine revealed open Amazon DynamoDB instances where anyone could download data or delete it entirely. They dug a little deeper and found Flowise, a connected open source GenAI development platform, where users can build AI agents powered by large language models (LLMs).

Roughly 90% of the nearly 3,000 scanned Flowise deployments were running unpatched versions vulnerable to a critical code-injection vulnerability, CVE-2024-58351 — and if the researchers could see it, so could attackers. So, Era Security deployed AI-powered honeypots, mostly on Amazon Web Service (AWS), to catch them in action.

Related:LLMjacking Emerges as Thriving Cybercriminal Enterprise

Honeypot technology is "not like it was before," and attackers don't recognize it's a decoy until they're much further into the environment, according to the Era Security research team. In the past, it took three or fourth months to build a realistic honeypot. Today, it's two hours.

"The most important finding wasn't the exploitation technique itself, but it was what happened after," Naor Talmor, chief technology officer (CTO) at Era Security, tells Dark Reading. What ensued was a repeatable, staged methodology where attackers mapped out privileges and the environment, hunted for credentials, exploited cloud metadata services across multiple providers to escalate access, and then locked in persistence, he explains. Attackers also obfuscated their activity by leaving seemingly benign and fleeting "hello" messages in the logs, making detection much more difficult because it requires team to spend time searching for specific indicators of compromise in the logs. Thus, the researchers gave the campaign the "Greeter" moniker.

The sophisticated techniques and persistence show that Greeter isn't an opportunistic smash-and-grab attack, a script kiddie, or someone who had merely stumbled upon the honeypots, the researchers noted. And, the campaign was deployed across several AWS regions at once, which indicates broader scale and intent.

Related:External Teams Chats Anchor 15-Minute Malware Delivery

Era Security warns of the potential scale the campaign could reach, confirming 46 real-world victims so far, based on the number of unauthenticated instances that mirrored the honeypot conditions. The team estimates that at least 50% of those remain connected to the Internet and visible, suggesting at least 20 organizations are currently exposed. The sophistication levels of the the threat actors also speaks to how how valuable the cloud and AI attack surface has become, given the effort required to launch Greeter; this wasn't low-hanging fruit.

The Greeter campaign signals a few shifts across the cloud landscape, including how much easier it's gotten for attackers to go unnoticed in workflows. Much of that is due to AI, which threat actors are using to assist in writing code or orchestrating attack steps, according to the Greeter attack logs.

A decade ago, cloud attacks targeted the Docker API. Threat actors then began exploiting workloads, escaping containers to reach the cloud's metadata server underneath, and exploiting service accounts from there, the researchers explain. While attackers are following a similar pattern, they are now using AI infrastructure as the new way to establish persistence.

Related:TiDB Breach Exposes Flawed Cloud Security Assumptions

Researchers agree that the end goal on each instance was to grab cloud credentials and perform lateral movement within a targeted organization.

Meanwhile, attribution remains quite challenging. When the researchers drilled down to the code and techniques, they started to see Chinese characters, though that could be a false flag. Still, a nation-state culprit would make since, because the notion of seeing an "entire orchestration, not even a big cybercrime group could execute that," according to Era Security.

The current velocity of vulnerability exploitation is nothing like the researchers have seen in the past, which puts immense pressure on organizations' traditional patching cycles. This played out just over the past few weeks alone, as security teams raced to fix PHP2Shell and OpenSSH, searching workloads one by one for flaws. However, the Greeter campaign shows a defense challenge for cloud security that's bigger than fixing any specific vulnerability.

Everyone needs to assume a breach now, warns Talmor, adding how if it's not a vulnerability, it may be leaked credentials or an exposed API. Organizations need to design security programs around the cloud to be ready for all of the above, he advises, adopting defense-in-depth.

"A lot of companies adopt that, but they don't really practice it," he notes.

The researchers say organizations can detect this kind of attack much faster if teams monitor for enumeration activity to close gaps before attackers find them. When applications or chat tools are installed, logging can be activated and streamed to a separate location so that even if attackers do delete a device's local history, the logs remain stored in the cloud.

Researchers also noted that this should be monitored closely in critical environments, especially where crown jewels or critical applications are involved, adding that organizations can have dozens or hundreds of accounts depending on the size.

"To be clear, we aren't saying, 'Stop caring about misconfigurations or stop caring about vulnerabilities," Talmor says, warning that patch management is a game "we are losing" that requires organizations to secure their environment layer by layer.

She previously lived in Florida where she wrote for the Tampa Bay Times before returning to Boston where her cybersecurity career took off at TechTarget SearchSecurity. When she's not writing about cybersecurity, she pursues personal projects that include a mystery novel and poetry collection.

Japan Pushes Emergency Funding for Cybersecurity as AI-Assisted Breaches Rise

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

The package adds public-private cyber responders, an AI-focused team run by the economic ministry, and more leeway to use the government's new active cyber-defense powers.

With Japan facing a surge in cyberattacks and breaches, the Japanese government has passed a ¥10 billion ($63 million) emergency cybersecurity package aimed at establishing teams of industry and government experts to quickly respond to attacks and better secure its infrastructure.

The four pillars of the strategy include thoroughly implementing basic cybersecurity measures country-wide, including adopting AI for cyber-defense, strengthening the nation's cyber-investigations capabilities, better supporting the victims of cyberattacks, and partnering with private industry to jointly respond to cyberattacks.

Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi called for the cybersecurity measures to be implemented "promptly and effectively" to enhance the nation's cyber resilience.

"Even at this very moment, incidents of unauthorized access and information leaks continue to occur, significantly affecting people's daily lives and business activities," Takaichi said in a published statement announced the emergency package on November 6. "Let us mobilize the full resources of the Government to strengthen Japan's overall capabilities to respond to cyberattacks."

Related:UK Wades Into Tech Sovereignty Amid AI Policy Changes

A surge in cyberattacks has caused significant damage to Japanese companies and government agencies. In the first nine months of 2026, more than 600 cybersecurity incidents were publicly reported, surpassing the 594 incidents in all of 2025, according to data from TrendAI, a cybersecurity firm (formerly TrendMicro). The public reports are a conservative estimate: Japan's privacy regulator logged 2,094 breach incidents (original in Japanese) from April through mid-October, also above the total for all of fiscal 2025.

While AI has aided attackers elsewhere, TrendAI has not established a firm link between the increases and the use of AI by attackers, Ryan Flores, co-lead for threat research at TrendAI, a cybersecurity firm, said in a statement on the threats.

"Cyberattacks are increasing globally, and so are the breaches and data leaks as a result," he said. "While AI might not be directly involved, its ability to help the attackers find targets and automate their operations makes cyberattacks scalable. Organizations need to shrink their cyber exposure and close the vulnerabilities in order to lower the odds of being victimized."

Recent compromises include ransomware attacks on business, such as frozen food giant Nichirei and brewery and food giant Asahi, and data breaches targeting GMO Internet Group. While many of the attacks are criminal in nature, the worry is that the stolen information could be used by other criminal groups and nation-state actors against the Japanese public, says Mihoko Matsubara, chief cybersecurity strategist for Japanese telecom giant NTT.

Related:UK Warns Its Critical Infrastructure Faces Grave Risk

"If attackers can collect and aggregate enough personal information, they can build detailed profiles of Japanese citizens, from personal preferences to economic activities," she says. "That data could potentially be used for influence operations against Japan, such as targeted disinformation campaigns to manipulate public opinion or undermine trust in Japanese institutions."

The Japanese government intends to use the emergency funding to pursue four broad initiatives to improve its overall cybersecurity posture. Pointing out that many recent breaches could have been prevented by good hygiene, the government's first initiative is to ensure organizations are implementing basic cybersecurity measures, according to the published Japanese notes of the ministerial meeting.

The second major initiative is to improve investigation and information collection capabilities, allowing better analysis that can then be delivered to the public to better prepare them for attacks. Third, the government will strengthen its response to cyberattacks, establishing international partnerships and implementing active cyber-defense measures. Finally, the government plans to create the capability and infrastructure to support victims so that citizens can recover damages and businesses can remain operational.

Related:Third Serengeti Sweep Tops 1,400 Arrests as Investigations Mature

"Accessible consultation points and direct expert support will be expanded so that affected organizations and individuals can connect to support without confusion, and communication to prevent secondary harm will be strengthened," the Japanese notes stated. "Work will also begin on building a nationwide mechanism for flexibly dispatching public- and private-sector specialists."

On October 1, Japan's Active Cyber Defense legislation came into effect, authorizing the police and the Self-Defense Forces (SDF) to take strong measures against any cyberattacks that pose a risk to Japan’s national security.

"Many countries would be keen to learn how quickly Japan could respond to which kind of cyberattacks, and with what level of intensity," says NTT's Matsubara, adding: "This emergency package is ... necessary to translate the law’s framework into practical capabilities across the broader economy, preparing organizations of all sizes to prevent, detect and respond to attacks as they become increasingly likely."

However, the funds are only enough to get started, and more will be necessary to continue Japan's investment in cybersecurity, she says.

"The important aspect about this package is that it recognizes that cybersecurity cannot be left entirely to individual organizations," Matsubara says. "Government assistance, in the form of security assessments and incident response or recovery support, can make a difference."

Rob is an award-winning, veteran technology journalist of more than 30 years, reporting on global cybersecurity issues, the latest offensive and defensive technologies, malware incidents, cyber conflict, and AI's impact on software and cybersecurity.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Recent reports include analyses of the shortage in cybersecurity workers, annual vulnerability trends, and annual threat reports.

Rob holds degrees from Cornell University in Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (double major).

Chinese AI Project Scouts Spies in Taiwan Chip Industry

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

The CCP built a Big Data system to scrape reams of information and learn about individuals abroad, to potentially target and exploit them. Think Alice Huang Lite.

The Chinese government is keeping tabs on ethnic Chinese people abroad via an AI-powered Big Data mining program. In some cases, the program is helping the government design influence operations targeting high-ranking individuals in the artificial intelligence (AI) industry.

Since the AI boom began, world leaders have been feeling out how to exploit it to cement their power. While Washington's emphasis has been on shorter-term AI ventures — tracking immigrants and political protestors, identifying social services to cut, etc. — the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) has been building something more durable: a data center that facilitates gargantuan Web scraping from social media and other public sources, feeds the results into machine learning algorithms, and outputs actionable intelligence for the apparatchiks in Beijing.

That's according to documents leaked to China watchdog NetAskari. In a report shared with Dark Reading and Sky News UK ahead of publication, NetAskari detailed a program that balances straightforward government PR with unsavory surveillance. Sometimes the data gathering has been designed around Cambridge Analytica-style targeted propaganda campaigns. In more nefarious cases, it has been used to identify individuals in political and economic spheres of interest who might one day make good spies or unwitting assets in spy campaigns.

Related:Malicious Linux Implants Mimic Asian Mail Security Products

"A foreign nation with rather undemocratic tendencies is running an extensive, AI-charged analysis and information-scraping operation to find ways to influence other nations' internal social, economic, and political landscapes," a NetAskari analyst explains in an interview with Dark Reading over Signal. "That this system is particularly aiming at the Chinese diaspora, to see if [foreign citizens] can be used to further these ambitions, even if they might not be aware of it, is rather shocking."

On Nov. 8, NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang — one of the few most important people in the most important industry in the world — emailed his staff, admitting to an affair with a newly outed spy. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) agent, Alice Huang, redirected Nvidia semiconductors to China, where they're banned, and translated intelligence from the CEO to the CCP.

While the MSS ran Huang, the United Front Work Department (UFWD) took its own, less dramatic approach to the challenge of breaching Taiwan's semiconductor industry.

The UFWD's main game is influence operations, at home and abroad. according to NetAskari. Its mission includes gauging and shaping media narratives about China abroad, engaging Chinese diaspora communities — to their benefit or detriment, depending on the situation — and, in certain cases, attempting to gain influence over powerful or otherwise useful individuals and organizations in China and abroad.

Related:UAE, Saudi Arabia Face Onslaught of Increasingly Complex Cyberattacks

In this instance, the UFWD appears to have been scoping out ethnic Chinese individuals involved in the semiconductor industry. A leaked report titled "Profiles of Ethnic-Chinese Leading Talent in the Chip Field, Their Circle Characteristics, and Work Recommendations" identifies executives of the Taiwanese AI company Skymizer, high-performing students at a top Taiwanese research university, and an AI research center director at Microsoft, among others, as individuals of note. It then details strategies for getting closer to these VIPs, like "leverage non-official organizations — hometown associations, alumni associations, professional societies — for relationship-building and cooperation."

The system used to generate this report is called the "Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform."

The platform's brain is a 500-square-meter Big Data laboratory at Jinan University in Guangzhou, built to comfortably house rows of server racks and at least 100 employees.

Related:Cybercriminals Hack Brazilian Government Servers to Host Phishing Sites

Basically, it's built to ingest massive amounts of online information about overseas Chinese communities, identify people, relationships, and public sentiments, and convert it all into actionable reports for CCP leadership, according to NetAskari.

Social media provides much of the content. The platform scrapes liberally from platforms like Instagram, Facebook, and X, plus Chinese platforms like Weibo and WeChat. As a rule, the actual scrapers are run on machines located in foreign countries. As with Chinese hacking campaigns more broadly, the machines doing the crimes are also shielded behind rotating proxy networks. The goal of this obfuscation, one leaked document explains, is to “ensure the security of data collection and prevent the collection system from being traced and discovered" by targeted platforms.

The data then makes its way to China for processing. Foreign languages are translated into Chinese, keywords are extracted, and meaningful links drawn. UFWD then cross-references these data with preexisting records of notable people identified as being involved in the media, sciences, politics, etc., as well as blacklisted and "two-faced people." These two sources feed a "business system" — the layer that seems to draw meaningful connections about public sentiments, the results of influence operations, and more.

Sometimes, the final reports outputted by this multitiered system simply concern public relations trends. Sometimes they've been used to identify ethnic Chinese individuals of note around the world — be they Taiwanese engineers, social media influencers, or something in between — and unearth strategies for how to utilize them to further the ends of the Chinese Communist Party.

Overall, the NetAskari analyst describes the Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform as "a worldwide dragnet that uses open source intelligence (OSINT) and Big Data analysis to identify and find potential collaborators, partners, and affiliates that the UFWD can work with to further their goal [of] extending the Communist Party's influence abroad and creating a 'China-friendly' environment in other nations' political, economic, and social settings."

It's nothing the UFWD wasn't doing before, to be sure. Instead, the analyst adds, "What used to be done manually, at a much slower speed, is now supercharged via AI and modern technology."

That, ultimately, is the rub. The leaked documents contain only outdated references, from 2021, about how machine learning (ML) and natural language processing (NLP) algorithms fit into this picture. It's unclear whether, and how, more modern large language models have been incorporated into — and potentially supercharged — the system. The better the AI gets, the more wide-ranging and granular the Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform can become, with real consequences for people who may not know they are being targeted by a foreign nation.

"A surveillance state can't accept that its own citizens are escaping to other nations, outside the reach of Beijing's grasp. For the best part of the last 20 years, most people had the luxury to ignore this part of the Chinese-state. Often practical and physical limitations restricted Chinese organiations to expand too quickly abroad or even get a foothold. But with remote methods like the ones we have seen applied in the Big-Data center of Jinan University, these 'natural boundaries' don't apply anymore," the analysti laments.

"Pair it now with ever growing globalization, China's stepping out onto the world stage, and crises of democracy in many Western nations, and it is not hard to see why such operations should concern even average citizens in the US or Europe," he says. "Even if they have no personal ties to China."

Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

Inside an AI Attack Against a US Retailer

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

When hand-typed commands failed to breach a US retailer, the attacker turned to AI-generated scripts, achieving lateral movement in minutes.

A threat actor deployed an AI model during a stalled cyberattack and rescued the campaign, achieving lateral movement in the process.

The attack, detailed today by Symantec, targeted an unnamed US retail organization in September. The threat actor's hand-typed commands were repeatedly blocked, so the attacker switched to what appeared to be AI-generated commands and rapidly broke through the retailer's defenses. The attacker ultimately harvested credentials and achieved lateral movement within minutes.

Symantec believes AI was used because the timing between commands was more indicative of automation than direct hands-on keyboard. That said, the timing was such that investigators believe this activity was "more consistent with an operator relaying output to an AI tool than with an autonomous agent."

The security firm said this incident is one of dozens of AI-assisted attacks Symantec has investigated since May, though it added that activity was heavily concentrated around September.

Related:Artex: The AI Pen-Test Tool Hackers Turned Into a Weapon

Threat actors have utilized large language models (LLMs) in their attacks as long as the technology has been widely available. Early applications involved generating convincing phishing lures or conducting basic data reconnaissance, but recent evidence shows attackers deploying AI to execute end-to-end attacks, assist with malware development, and more. More recently, attackers have abused — and continue to abuse — agentic penetration testing tools to break into target environments at machine speed.

In this attack specifically, Symantec said it did not identify the initial access vector, focusing more on the timeline it was able to glean from its post-incident investigation. According to the blog post, the attacker worked at first by hand, which investigators determined due to artifacts like typing errors.

When every staged payload was terminated by security software, indicators suggest attacker tactics changed when they tried again the next day. "For almost two hours," Symantec said, "the attackers submitted long, error-free PowerShell scripts with labelled output sections and, in places, comments that read as a model's reasoning." Approximately 10 minutes after querying the victim's SolarWinds Orion monitoring software, attackers managed to laterally move to a second server.

The blog post hypothesizes that the attackers behind the attack were likely financially motivated, as they searched point-of-sale scripts for passwords, and one address they contacted was linked to an operation that stole card data from online retailers.

Related:CISA Announces Enhanced Reporting Form for Cyber Incidents

During the second-stage activity, the threat actor gained initial access to a Web server and stole credentials from configuration files tied to the SolarWinds Orion monitoring software. And while the idea of AI speeding up or improving attacker tradecraft is nothing new, the attack is its deployment of AI to a hands-on keyboard attack in progress and possibly seizing victory out of an apparent defeat. It presents LLMs as a difference-maker for a less skilled threat actor.

Dick O'Brien, principal intelligence analyst on the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, tells Dark Reading this incident is one of several the team has seen in recent weeks where AI revived an attack. While he calls this a game-changer for attackers, "defenders can potentially leverage that pattern" as AI behaves in a particular kind of way, such as testing variants of a technique in quick succession.

"The retry pattern is a signal itself," he says. "Blocking on behavior rather than file names or exact commands matters much more. Treating a blocked attempt followed by a variant as a high-priority alert is worth considering."

Although no vulnerability was exploited and no patching was required, O'Brien believes this trend of AI accelerating attacks is and will continue to affect the patching race. "Our view is that defenders need to start looking at mean time to mitigate rather than mean time to patch. Patching is still important, but it can’t be the only countermeasure."

Related:Operação de influência russa tem como alvo generalizado a América Latina

Who Runs the Ransomware Group Qilin?

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

The cybercrime group Qilin has emerged as the world’s most active ransomware affiliate program, claiming at least 365 victim organizations in the past three months alone. But even top Russian hackers make operational security mistakes, and the administrator of Qilin has made a few key missteps over the last decade that all point to a 36-year-old man living in St. Petersburg, Russia.

Operating since September 2022, the Russia-based ransomware group Qilin is the currently the most industrious ransomware-as-a-service gang, according to a report (PDF) this month by the cybersecurity firm NCC Group (KrebsOnSecurity profiled the administrator of the second most active ransomware group — The Gentlemen — in June).

The Qilin ransomware administrator “Haise” advertising his affiliate program on the cybercrime forum RAMP. Image: Ke-la.com.

Qilin attracts affiliates to hack into companies and deploy ransomware by offering them at least 80 percent of any extortion amount paid by victims. Its members practice the “double extortion” model, stealing sensitive data before encrypting systems and threatening to publish the files online. Unlike most ransomware partnerships based out of Russia, Qilin is happy to team with English-speaking affiliates, and its leader has shared on Telegram that they’ve worked with cybercrime groups like Scattered Spider and RansomHub.

The head of Qilin goes by the handle “Haise,” but they weren’t always known by this nickname. It is not uncommon for cybercriminals to change their handle periodically as an operational security (opsec) measure. Doing so is generally a smart move, because it helps to sever one’s ties to older identities that may not have been as careful on basic opsec, such as separating their real life identity from their cybercrime activities. But even those cautious criminals will sometimes have to reference their past monikers when applying for membership at a new forum. And forums eventually get hacked or seized. That is precisely what happened with Haise.

The cyber intelligence platform Intel 471 records that in June 2022, Haise applied for membership to RAMP, a closely-guarded Russian cybercrime forum that was created as a haven for ransomware affiliate programs. Intel 471 has indexed direct messages from RAMP (the forum was seized by the FBI in January 2026), and those missives show the RAMP administrator approved Haise’s application, referring to this user as Haise and by their Telegram username: Alexbigboss44.

Searching Intel 471 for Alexbigboss44 reveals this was the Telegram account name shared by the user Rjun on the Russian cybercrime forums Exploit and XSS two years earlier in 2020. The cyber intelligence platform Flashpoint finds Alexbigboss44 is a prolific participant in cybercriminal forums and Telegram channels, primarily focused on the illicit trade of traffic, logs, and corporate access [full disclosure: Flashpoint is an advertiser on this blog].

The Qilin administrator once used the nickname “Rjun” on several forums, and shared their Telegram handle as “Alexbigboss44.” Image: Ke-la.com.

Flashpoint says this user was engaged in high-volume acquisition of Facebook logs and Google Ads accounts to facilitate fraudulent advertising and gambling schemes, and that they functioned as a broker and operator — managing a network of traffickers and purchasing compromised data to fuel their operations.

The link between the Qilin administrator Haise and the much older Rjun and Alexbigboss44 accounts was shared privately earlier this month by IntelHawk, which describes itself as an AI-based platform capable of de-anonymizing threat actors based on historical usernames, stylometry and other signals.

IntelHawk founder and chief technology officer Leo Bogod said an analysis of these identities shows a progression from novice arbitrageur and credit card fraudster in 2018–2019 to a more experienced participant and organizer of complex cybercrime-related activities in 2020–2023, with increasing attention to malware, botnets, and payment infrastructure.

Where does this connection between these threat actor identities lead? Intel 471 finds that by the time Alexbigboss44 was applying for membership to RAMP, Rjun was already a longtime member of several other Russian crime forums, including Darkmarket, Verified, and Zloy. Intel 471 says Rjun signed up at all of these sites with the email address mail-30a@mail.ru.

The identity threat protection firm Spycloud found the email address mail-30a@mail.ru at one time used the password “Rjun132” [Spycloud is also an advertiser on this blog]. A search for that email at the breach tracking service Constella Intelligence shows that it appeared in records maintained by gosuslugi.ru, the official digital platform for state and municipal services in the Russian Federation.

Gosuslugi was breached in late 2025, reportedly exposing more than 20 million records with full names, birth dates, and mobile numbers. According to the leaked Gosuslugi records (via Constella), the address mail-30a@mail.ru belongs to 36-year-old Aleksey Mikhailovich Yakovtsev from St. Petersburg, Russia.

Flashpoint finds Haise has long used a Telegram account with the user ID 118329521, recording more than 5,000 messages from this user since 2018. Records from Russia’s education watchdog Rosobrnadzor reveal that Mr. Yakovtsev went to grade school in Murmansk and that he holds a master’s degree in computing and information systems.

A search on this Telegram ID in Constella shows it is linked to the Russian phone number +79602347275. Pivoting on that phone number in Constella reveals it is connected to the email addresses aleksey1990yakov@mail.ru and rjun123321@mail.ru. IntelHawk finds that +79602347275 belongs to Yakovtsev’s biological sister, and that Aleksey often used his sister’s email address when registering at different websites.

That phone number appears on several airline flight records at Russian travel agencies that have experienced a data breach in recent years, and those records show Aleksey Yakovtsev, born August 18, 1990, flew between St. Petersburg and Murmansk at least three times between 2013 and 2014.

More recent flight records show that while Mr. Yakovtsev has not traveled by plane outside of Russia, he does fly frequently between Saint Petersburg and Sochi. Often called the “Russian Riviera,” Sochi is Russia’s largest resort city, situated along the coast of the Black Sea and about 1,200 miles south of Saint Petersburg by plane.

Attempts to reach Mr. Yakovtsev via email and phone were unsuccessful.

Further reading: GangExposed, an anonymous Twitter/X account that has a history of successfully deanonymizing suspected ransomware actors, reached similar conclusions about Haise’s real-life identity, crediting IntelHawk’s platform for helping to connect the dots.

The Qilin ransomware admin Haise’s now-deleted Telegram account, advertising the affiliate program in July 2024. Image: justpaste.it/iosba.
