ZIG Finance Opens $100M Vault to Bring Saudi Arabia's SME Lending Boom Onchain

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

AI, Web3, Cybersecurity, Startup Funding & Enterprise SaaS. Top Journalist, Analyst & Thought Leadership Media Leader.

AI, Web3, Cybersecurity, Startup Funding & Enterprise SaaS. Top Journalist, Analyst & Thought Leadership Media Leader.

AI, Web3, Cybersecurity, Startup Funding & Enterprise SaaS. Top Journalist, Analyst & Thought Leadership Media Leader.

Private credit, the business of lending to companies outside the public bond market and the banking system, has grown into a market of more than $2 trillion that Moody's expects to approach $4 trillion by 2030. A growing slice of it now lives onchain. Private credit makes up about 44% of the $51 billion market for tokenized real-world assets, which puts roughly $22 billion of loans on public blockchains, while onchain private credit grew about 180% in a year to early 2026.

Saudi Arabia is one of the fastest-growing credit markets feeding that demand. Lending to Saudi small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) reached SR468 billion, or about $124.6 billion, at the end of 2025, up 33% in a year, while Vision 2030 aims to lift SMEs' share of total credit from 11.5% to 20%. Global institutions have had few direct routes into those loans, since most are made by local banks and lending platforms.

ZIG Finance launched the zEMS Vault on 11 November 2026 to open that route. The vault gives eligible institutional investors exposure to a diversified pool of Saudi SME private credit on BNB Chain, with subscriptions and redemptions in stablecoins. It is the first product from ZIG Finance's strategic partnership with Laser Digital, the digital assets subsidiary of Japanese bank Nomura, which acts as risk framework advisor, while the zEMS onchain strategy targets a raise of $100 million.

Private credit has been one of the fastest-growing asset classes since the 2008 financial crisis, as banks pulled back from riskier lending and specialist funds stepped in. Moody's puts global assets under management at more than $2 trillion, with a forecast of close to $4 trillion by 2030, as insurers, pension funds and wealthy investors look for income above public bond yields.

Tokenization, the process of recording ownership of an asset as a digital token on a blockchain, is bringing that market to new investors. Private credit overtook US Treasury products in 2026 to become the largest category of tokenized real-world assets, at about 44% of the market against 30% for Treasuries, with the total market up 42% so far in 2026. Onchain private credit products have typically offered yields of about 8% to 12%, compared with 4% to 5% for tokenized Treasuries, which explains the appetite.

Saudi lending to SMEs has grown at one of the fastest rates of any major credit market. Credit to micro, small and medium enterprises rose from SR307.4 billion in the second quarter of 2024 to SR351.7 billion by the end of that year, then to SR420.7 billion by mid-2025 and SR468 billion by the end of 2025. That is a 52% rise in 18 months (our arithmetic), while SMEs' share of total credit climbed from about 9.6% to 11.5%.

The growth is driven by Vision 2030, Saudi Arabia's economic plan, which aims to lift the SME share of credit to 20% and SMEs' contribution to GDP to 35%. Banks provide about 95% of SME credit today, with financing companies providing the rest, which leaves wide room for non-bank lenders and outside investors as the target approaches. Reaching 20% from 11.5% means SME credit has to keep growing faster than total lending for years.

Private credit is becoming a major source of growth capital across the Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC), the six-country bloc that includes Saudi Arabia and the UAE. Private debt deployed in the region grew from about $0.5 billion in 2024 to $4.1 billion in 2025, overtaking venture capital at $3.3 billion, while Saudi Arabia accounted for about $3.9 billion of the total.

The demand is far larger than the supply. GCC SMEs face a credit gap of more than $250 billion, with SMEs receiving less than 10% of bank lending across the region, while global managers including King Street have announced plans to deploy billions in Saudi private credit. Industry estimates expect Gulf private credit to grow 15% to 30% a year over the next five years, as Fortune has noted in its coverage of the region.

The vault is issued by a segregated portfolio of ZM Structured Products SPC Limited, a company in the British Virgin Islands whose segregated portfolios keep each product's assets and liabilities separate. Merritt Administrators, which trades as ZIG Finance and is an authorized financial services provider regulated by South Africa's Financial Sector Conduct Authority, acts as curator. Investors hold vault tokens and do not own the underlying loans directly.

Each partner covers one part of the chain. Beehive originates the loans, Zamanat structures the product and provides Sharia advisory so it can meet Islamic finance principles, Laser Digital supports the design of the risk governance framework and gives tokenization advice, while ZIG Markets handles issuance, curation and distribution on BNB Chain. KAIO, the tokenization platform built by Laser Digital and WebN, runs tokenization, compliance and lifecycle management, including know-your-customer (KYC) checks at subscription and redemption.

The structure is also expected to include Khalij as the Sharia certification partner, with an independent calculation agent to be appointed separately. A Sharia-aligned structure opens the vault to investors who follow Islamic finance rules, a pool that the Islamic Financial Services Board puts at about $4.4 trillion of industry assets in 2025.

Beehive brings the lending base. Founded in Dubai in 2014, it was the first peer-to-peer lending platform in the Middle East and North Africa to be regulated by the Dubai Financial Services Authority. It has funded more than $1 billion for more than 2,100 SMEs across the UAE, Saudi Arabia and Oman. Its Saudi entity is subject to regulatory approval, while its UAE and Oman businesses are regulated by the DFSA and Oman's FSA.

KAIO brings the onchain rails. The platform has transacted more than $500 million in volume across more than 10 blockchains, with institutional funds from partners including BlackRock, Brevan Howard, Hamilton Lane, Laser Digital and Mubadala Capital live on it. Laser Digital, which Nomura set up to bridge traditional and crypto markets, is regulated by Dubai's VARA and ADGM's FSRA and registered with Japan's FSA.

ZIG Finance ties the pieces together. Its ecosystem includes ZIGChain, a layer 1 blockchain built for institutional finance, ZIG Markets as its distribution layer and the ZIG token. Laser Digital became a strategic investor and risk partner in ZIGChain in August 2026, with the two companies setting a target of at least $100 million in total value locked across several onchain credit products.

The vault launches on BNB Chain, one of the most active networks for tokenized assets. Real-world assets on the chain grew from about $3 billion in March 2026 to $5.2 billion in July, a 73% rise in four months (our arithmetic), making it the second-largest RWA network behind Ethereum with a 14.9% share.

The vault's design is about managing credit risk in the open. ZIG Finance's stated aim is to make the risk of lending to Saudi SMEs transparent and manageable, so institutional investors can get comfortable with an asset class they could not otherwise access at scale. Laser Digital's role is to help design ZIG Finance's own risk framework and the tokenization and issuance structure, with responsibility for credit selection and risk management resting with ZIG Finance and the originator.

The product carries the risks of any private credit investment. The vault does not guarantee returns, is not principal-protected and is not a deposit, while credit risk remains tied to the underlying SME borrowers. Laser Digital and Nomura do not guarantee, underwrite or rate the loans. The product is available only to eligible institutional investors in selected jurisdictions, excluding US persons and residents of Canada and Australia.

The zEMS Vault is the first product milestone in ZIG Finance's partnership with Laser Digital, which targets a minimum of $100 million in total value locked across multiple product rollouts. The companies describe the vault's architecture as a template that can be applied to other emerging markets and credit strategies, combining local origination with institutional risk governance, compliant tokenization and broader distribution.

Further opportunities across the region and other asset classes are already under consideration. A $100 million raise would equal about 10% of the more than $1 billion Beehive has funded since 2014 and about 0.08% of Saudi SME credit outstanding (our arithmetic), which shows how much room the model has to grow if it works.

Saudi SME credit is growing by tens of billions of riyals a year, Gulf private debt grew eightfold in 2025 and tokenized private credit has become the largest category of onchain real-world assets. The zEMS Vault sits where those three trends meet, giving global institutions a regulated, Sharia-aligned and stablecoin-settled route into a market that has mostly been funded by local banks.

The first test is the $100 million raise and how the loan pool performs through its first cycles. If the structure gains traction, ZIG Finance and Laser Digital plan to apply the same model to more GCC and emerging-market credit, turning a single vault into a pipeline that connects local lenders with global onchain capital.

Beyond Fiber Optics: The Hardware Reality of a Decentralized Internet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Tech enthusiast & writer exploring AI algorithms, quant models, and decentralized technology.

Tech enthusiast & writer exploring AI algorithms, quant models, and decentralized technology.

Tech enthusiast & writer exploring AI algorithms, quant models, and decentralized technology.

In its 2023 report, the digital rights group Access Now documented 283 internet shutdowns across 39 countries. While this figure includes targeted platform blocking rather than just total national blackouts, it highlights a stark reality: various state actors and conflicting parties hold the ultimate trump card. They can simply issue administrative orders to local telecom operators to restrict routing or suspend connectivity.

Web3 developers have spent years bulletproofing consensus mechanisms and peer-to-peer storage. Yet, if the physical fiber-optic cables belong to a state monopoly, software encryption cannot protect network availability. Furthermore, even when physical lines remain active, governments can compel ISPs to execute software-level blocks like SNI (Server Name Indication) filtering. Whether through severing physical connections or enforcing digital filters at the ISP level, access to even the most decentralized dApp becomes severely restricted for average users.

This physical bottleneck highlights the core motivation behind Decentralized Physical Infrastructure Networks (DePIN). Projects in the satellite space, including Spacecoin, are engineering architectures to decouple network access from terrestrial telecoms. However, bypassing legacy ISPs is not an absolute cure for censorship—it merely shifts the battleground from software firewalls to hardware logistics.

Many assume that orbital networks bypass terrestrial laws entirely, but Starlink’s recent legal battle in Brazil illustrates the limitations of centralized physical infrastructure.

After the Brazilian Supreme Court ordered X (formerly Twitter) blocked, Starlink initially refused to comply. After Justice Alexandre de Moraes froze Starlink's local accounts over potential liability for fines owed by X, Starlink notified the telecom regulator that it would comply with the court's blocking order. While Starlink publicly maintained that the asset freeze was illegal, this chronological sequence of events underscores how operating as a centralized corporate entity with a vulnerable financial footprint creates distinct compliance chokepoints.

Web3 infrastructure projects aim to solve this by separating the coordination protocol from physical data routing. However, it is crucial to distinguish between protocol decentralization and physical hardware ownership. According to deployment updates, Spacecoin's CTC-0 nanosatellite launched in December 2024, followed by three CTC-1 satellites in November 2025. These satellites are physically owned and operated by a traditional corporate entity (Space Telecommunications Inc.).

The Spacecoin protocol does not decentralize the physical satellite ownership. Instead, its technical roadmap outlines a separation of capabilities. According to Spacecoin's official updates, its protocol is designed to manage authorization, authentication, and accounting across the decentralized infrastructure, while smart contracts and crypto payments support bandwidth coordination. However, the extent to which these capabilities are operational in the deployed satellite network remains distinct from the protocol's stated design.

On October 1, 2025, Spacecoin claimed a successful demonstration using its CTC-0 satellite to transmit secured data over 7,000 kilometers between Chile and the Azores. While Spacecoin has since announced regional connectivity pilots and commercial partnerships in 2026, a globally resilient, independently accessible satellite internet service remains a different milestone from its initial proofs of concept.

Treating orbital networks as an instant bypass for censorship ignores how physical infrastructure is regulated. Moving the internet to an orbital mesh introduces distinct vectors for state control.

Space is borderless, but RF (Radio Frequency) spectrum is strictly policed. Transmitting high-speed data to orbit typically involves licensed spectrum, such as Ku, Ka, L, or S bands. Legally utilizing these frequencies requires two distinct regulatory processes: international coordination through the ITU for frequency assignments and orbital positions—including procedures intended to prevent harmful interference—and separate national authorizations granted by local communications regulators within their jurisdictions. If a decentralized project operates outside authorized spectrum allocations or exceeds the technical limits applicable to license-exempt ISM bands, state actors may treat the transmissions as unlawful. Governments do not necessarily need to attack the blockchain; they can confiscate unauthorized hardware or, in specific scenarios, attempt to deploy localized radio jamming equipment to disrupt targeted frequencies.

While downloading data via a passive receiver can sometimes go unnoticed, two-way satellite internet requires user terminals to transmit data back to orbit. These uplink transmissions emit RF signatures that, with specialized equipment, can potentially be detected and localized. Regimes can outlaw the import and possession of unauthorized satellite terminals. If customs agencies seize the hardware at the border, it can severely restrict network deployment before a single node comes online.

Launching nanosatellites and manufacturing custom RF receivers requires massive upfront capital. If a decentralized network relies on a small number of aerospace launch providers or hardware manufacturers to deploy its infrastructure, those companies become the new chokepoints. A state actor merely needs to apply regulatory pressure on the hardware manufacturers or impose import restrictions to cripple the network's expansion.

Decentralized physical infrastructure does not erase state control. Instead, it forces a shift in the logistics of executing it.

Today, restricting internet access is a relatively straightforward administrative task that relies on legal mandates enforced through local regulators, government agencies, and compliant telecom operators. Censoring a blockchain-coordinated satellite network, however, expands the required enforcement toolkit. It creates a scenario where a state might be forced to strictly police domestic hardware supply chains and import channels, actively track localized RF uplinks, or seize physical terminals.

A decentralized physical internet does not stop governments from using inexpensive software blocks; rather, it introduces a reality where regimes may have to supplement digital firewalls with highly visible, financially exhausting physical enforcement.

Tech enthusiast & writer exploring AI algorithms, quant models, and decentralized technology.

SkyW4lker Exposed: A Growing Microsoft 365 Phishing Threat to American Enterprises

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Online interactive sandbox for DFIR/SOC investigations. Fast malware analysis and cybersecurity threat detection.

Online interactive sandbox for DFIR/SOC investigations. Fast malware analysis and cybersecurity threat detection.

Online interactive sandbox for DFIR/SOC investigations. Fast malware analysis and cybersecurity threat detection.

SkyW4lker (SkyW4lker x365 Sentinel) is an active device-code phishing kit targeting Microsoft 365 users in the United States, Europe, and other regions. By abusing Microsoft’s legitimate authentication process, the kit can give attackers access to corporate accounts without stealing passwords or bypassing MFA. This puts business emails, sensitive documents, and other Microsoft 365 resources at risk of unauthorized access.

The ANY.RUN team identified 618 sandbox analyses related to SkyW4lker between June and October 21, 2026, with 46% of submissions coming from the United States. Activity increased from 20 analyses in June to 200 in September, highlighting the growing exposure of organizations relying on Microsoft 365.

JavaScript obfuscation (obfuscator.io), Device-Code phishing, Geolocation detection, IP-address detection, CAPTCHA verification

Email attachments (.pdf, .docx, .xls/xlsx and others), redirection links and lure pages (including Google Maps, Google Images, Miro, Zoom and others)

URL patterns: /session_public.php?action= /endpoints/confirm_visit.php /js/url_helper.js /js/endpoint.js /js/client_geo.js

The United States accounts for 47% of SkyW4lker-related sandbox submissions, more than twice the share of any other country. With the campaign exploiting legitimate Microsoft authentication processes and routine business communications, these findings raise concerns for US enterprises about how effectively existing security controls can identify suspicious activity before it leads to account compromise.

SkyW4lker highlights several security risks that organizations need to account for when protecting business-critical systems and employee identities:

Stop account compromise before it disrupts your business.Detect threats earlier and reduce response costs.

You can examine the full SkyW4lker attack chain in this ANY.RUN Sandbox analysis.

The attacker sends the victim a phishing email with a password-protected attachment. Typical subjects involve commercial offers, contracts, invoices, or confidential documents. The attachment is a PDF, DOCX, or XLSX file, and the password is provided in the email body.

The document contains a link to a CAPTCHA page. Some variants include an additional lure page hosted on a legitimate service such as Miro, Google Sites, or Zoom, which redirects the victim to the next step.

Finally, the victim is directed to a phishing page that instructs them to enter a device code on Microsoft’s legitimate authentication page, potentially granting the attacker access to their Microsoft 365 account.

The phishing page relies on obfuscated JavaScript functions and separate script files. Using ANY.RUN’s In-Browser Data Inspection, analysts can examine the page’s DOM and HTTP activity to understand how these scripts operate.

The scripts keep the page in regular contact with the PHP backend and determine the location of the victim from their IP address. Within the page DOM, the main script loads additional modules, sets click handlers on buttons and builds GET requests to the PHP endpoint /session_public.php with the action parameter (start or poll).

With action=start, the script sends the geolocation and IP address of the victim and receives a session ID. After that, it sends a request with action=poll every few seconds to check whether the victim has authenticated.

The other modules of the phishing page are listed below:

Determines the location of the victim from the response of one of three services: ip-api[.]com, ipapi[.]co or ipwho[.]is. The location can also be obtained through the PHP endpoint php/endpoints/client_geo.php.

Base script with helper wrappers for URL handling, such as building GET parameters and appending slashes.

Tracks the state of the victim. It logs the e-mail address, password, browser, MFA status, country and other auxiliary fields used during authentication and sends them to the PHP backend at php/endpoints/accounts.php.

A single phishing URL can be the starting point for uncovering an entire campaign. In the SkyW4lker investigation, identifying the malicious page was only the beginning. Analysts needed to trace related activity, identify additional infrastructure, and determine how far the campaign extended. Traditionally, connecting these findings requires repeated IOC lookups, manual correlation, and searching through previous investigations.

ANY.RUN’s major new upgrade, Related Threat Context, makes this process significantly faster and easier. By bringing connections to previous sandbox investigations and threat intelligence directly into the Interactive Sandbox, it helps analysts discover related threats, reuse existing findings, and expand investigations with fewer manual steps.

With more than 700,000 security professionals and 16,000 organizations worldwide using ANY.RUN, analysts can draw on a vast pool of real-world investigation data to uncover connections that might otherwise take considerably longer to find.

During our SkyW4lker investigation, the new Related analyses section surfaced previous sandbox sessions sharing the same URL patterns. This gave analysts an immediate starting point for tracing related activity instead of manually searching for connections across individual investigations.

Using the distinctive PHP endpoint /session_public.php, we moved into ANY.RUN Threat Intelligence Lookup in a single click, discovered additional phishing activity, and expanded the campaign cluster.

The investigation demonstrates the practical value of Related Threat Context: less time spent searching for connections, faster discovery of related infrastructure, and more evidence available to guide the next investigation step.

The same approach can be applied to other suspicious URLs and indicators, allowing SOC teams to build on previous investigations and uncover related threat infrastructure faster.

Tier 1 analysts spend less time on repetitive IOC research, gaining more evidence to support accurate triage and escalation decisions. Tier 2 analysts receive richer investigation context, helping them focus on cases that genuinely require deeper analysis.

By connecting individual alerts to existing threat intelligence, Related Threat Context helps SOC teams shorten investigation cycles, reduce unnecessary manual work, and make better-informed response decisions.

Give your SOC the context they need for faster triage.Cut repetitive investigations. Increase Tier 1 productivity.

Each phishing page has its own backend, so the administration panel is likely to be hosted on the same domain. Further analysis of the Threat Intelligence Lookup results and data from other sources revealed an administration panel at /admin/index.php.

The CSS styles (command-center.css) suggest the following capabilities of the kit:

Further investigation revealed another important finding: SkyW4lker shares several technical components with Gabagool, a credential phishing kit.

A search in ANY.RUN Threat Intelligence for the unique module paths of SkyW4lker url:”/js/client_geo.js” or url:”/js/url_helper.js” or url:”/js/endpoint.js”) also returns sandbox sessions tagged gabagool. Gabagool was first observed on 23 April 2026, two months before SkyW4lker, and has used the endpoint.js and url_helper.js modules since late April and early May. The two kits share several other features:

client_geo.js, url_helper.js, endpoint.js (since July 2026)

client_geo.js, url_helper.js, endpoint.js (since late April 2026)

/assets/private_captcha/public/widget.js?v=4

/assets/private_captcha/public/widget.js?v=4

Moreover, some sandbox sessions tagged gabagool load the kit from directories named skywalker:

goldcreekconcrete[.]com/example/skywalker/ (sandbox session) and goldcreekconcrete[.]com/skywalker/example/ (sandbox session). On goldcreekconcrete[.]org, which also hosted Gabagool (sandbox session), the directory /skyw4lker/skydirectory/ with the same modules appeared on 29 September (sandbox session).

We therefore assess with high confidence that Gabagool and SkyW4lker are built by the same developer. Gabagool harvests logins and passwords through accounts.php, whereas SkyW4lker abuses the device-code flow through session_public.php, so we regard them as two attack modes of one platform. The identical directories on shared hosts may also point to a common operator; however, the available data does not allow us to distinguish one operator from several customers of the same platform.

The SkyW4lker investigation reveals several technical indicators and attack patterns that security teams can use to identify related phishing activity. For organizations, these findings also highlight opportunities to strengthen identity security and reduce the impact of potential account compromise.

Hunt for SkyW4lker’s technical fingerprints. Look for requests to /session_public.php?action=start and repeated /session_public.php?action=poll calls. Check the JavaScript modules and URL patterns identified in this research to uncover related infrastructure.

Connect phishing activity with suspicious sign-ins. Monitor Microsoft Entra ID logs for unexpected device-code authentication, particularly following suspicious emails or document-sharing requests. Correlating authentication events with phishing indicators can help identify potential account compromise.

Expand investigations beyond individual IOCs. ANY.RUN’s newly introduced Related Threat Context puts connected sandbox analyses at analysts’ fingertips, with a one-click pivot to threat intelligence context for deeper threat hunting. In the SkyW4lker investigation, this helped expand the campaign cluster, while further analysis revealed shared infrastructure and code similarities with Gabagool.

Investigate potential follow-on activity. When account compromise is suspected, review subsequent Microsoft 365 activity to identify affected resources, unauthorized access, and possible attempts to target other users.

Give Tier 1 the evidence to make faster decisions.Keep more cases at Tier 1 with 30% fewer escalations.

SkyW4lker demonstrates why organizations need to look beyond traditional phishing prevention and MFA when evaluating identity security. Three priorities deserve attention:

Close authentication policy gaps. Review where device-code authentication is genuinely required. Restrict or block unnecessary use through Microsoft Entra Conditional Access to reduce opportunities for attackers to abuse legitimate authentication flows.

Strengthen detection across email and identity systems. Ensure the SOC can connect suspicious attachments, phishing infrastructure, and unusual authentication events. Better visibility across these stages helps teams identify potential compromises before attackers expand their access.

Reduce investigation time and escalation costs. Equip Tier 1 analysts with historical threat context and clear escalation criteria. Establish procedures for containing compromised accounts and reviewing unauthorized activity, helping Tier 2 teams focus on incidents requiring deeper investigation.

SkyW4lker continues to evolve, with new endpoints, changing URL patterns, and growing activity across sandbox analyses. Its connection to Gabagool suggests that the same developer is supporting both device-code and credential phishing, giving attackers different ways to compromise business accounts through shared infrastructure.

For organizations, the findings highlight a broader security challenge: attackers are increasingly exploiting legitimate authentication processes and trusted business services, making malicious activity harder to distinguish from normal operations.

As phishing kits evolve, security teams need more than traditional email filtering and MFA. Stronger identity controls, visibility into suspicious authentication activity, and access to connected threat context can help SOCs identify attacks earlier, investigate them faster, and reduce the potential impact on business operations.

T1589.002 Gather Victim Identity Information: Email Addresses

The SkyW4lker operator collected recipient e-mail addresses to personalise phishing links using the landing-URL suffix ? or ?? after /it[A-Za-z0-9]{32}us.

T1590.005 Gather Victim Network Information: IP Addresses

The SkyW4lker phishing kit recorded the visitor's IP address, ISP, organisation and location to profile victims using client_geo.js and the request /assets/php/endpoints/session_public.php?action=start&ip=…&isp=…&org=…&org_type=.

T1583.001 Acquire Infrastructure: Domains

The SkyW4lker operator registered look-alike domains of real companies to host phishing pages using typo-squatted names under Microsoft-themed subdomain chains, e.g. sdocshares.auth.outlook2026common.copilos26.csr-rooflng[.]com and pdocshared.payments2026.outlooks.common.ecmholdlnggroup[.]com.

T1583.001 Acquire Infrastructure: Domains

The SkyW4lker operator obtained free dynamic-DNS subdomains to host phishing pages using flow505.mooo[.]com, bigbrain.chickenkiller[.]com and off365acess.jumpingcrab[.]com.

T1583.003 Acquire Infrastructure: Virtual Private Server

The SkyW4lker operator rented virtual private servers to run the PHP backend using DigitalOcean hosting (50 of 92 backend IP addresses), e.g. 162.243.64[.]121 and 67.205.154[.]32.

T1608.005 Stage Capabilities: Link Target

The SkyW4lker operator staged intermediate pages to forward victims to the kit using public cloud buckets and site builders, e.g. pub-177b3aa5a54d4f9087a649de5ec766d5.r2[.]dev/docsonedrive2026.html, carcabaoperadologistca.fr-par-1.linodeobjects[.]com/document_excel.html and bethom-contracting-llc.mintlify[.]site.

T1566.001 Phishing: Spearphishing Attachment

The SkyW4lker operator sent password-protected attachments to deliver the phishing link using PDF, XLS, DOCX and EML lures named -protected.pdf or DOC <10 random characters>.pdf, e.g. SHA256 9aa0b87974b9b4cd42603833b7ad44257578467d279b315a071108a5ed02c268.

The SkyW4lker operator sent links hosted on legitimate services to pass e-mail reputation checks using Google Sites, Axure Cloud and Smartsheet pages and the maps.google[.]ie/url?q= redirect, e.g. sites.google[.]com/heatherrasekhomes.com/talbert-manufacturing-inc-0049/home.

The SkyW4lker phishing kit relied on the victim opening the landing link to start a phishing session using the path //it[A-Za-z0-9]{32}us.

The SkyW4lker operator imitated routine business correspondence to make the lures credible using subjects such as New Submission From , Approved Statement and Fedwire Funds Service Processed-()-.

The SkyW4lker phishing kit placed a CAPTCHA in front of the phishing page to hide it from automated scanners using Cloudflare Turnstile and the self-hosted /assets/private_captcha/public/widget.js?v=4 with /assets/php/endpoints/private_captcha_challenge.php.

The SkyW4lker phishing kit obfuscated its JavaScript to hinder analysis using obfuscator.io-style code (520 of 618 analyses carry the obfuscated-js tag).

The SkyW4lker phishing kit abused the Microsoft OAuth 2.0 device-code flow to obtain the victim's access tokens using login.microsoft[.]com/device and login.microsoftonline[.]com/common/oauth2/deviceauth while polling /assets/php/endpoints/session_public.php?action=poll&session_id=.

T1056.003 Input Capture: Web Portal Capture

The SkyW4lker phishing kit logged the victim's e-mail, password, browser, MFA status and country to collect credentials using endpoint.js and the backend php/endpoints/accounts.php.

The best Thanksgiving baking tips from Ina Garten, Erin McDowell, and more pro bakers: Top tips from the Things Bakers Know podcast.

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

It’s the most wonderful baking season of the year, and to help you bake your best, we have top tips from some of the country’s best bakers, pulled from conversations on the Things Bakers Know podcast. Below, you’ll find Thanksgiving strategies from upcoming podcast guests Ina Garten (episode dropping November 23!) and King Arthur’s Martin Philip (find his episode November 16), as well as guests from previously aired episodes, including Erin McDowell, Helen Goh, and many more of our baking friends.

“The one thing I think people think they can do is multiply a recipe times four and think it’s going to come out fine, which it won’t,” Ina shared on her forthcoming podcast episode. Instead of taking one recipe and just multiplying by three or four, she suggests either baking the original recipe multiple times, or doubling it and baking two or three times, depending on how many people you’re feeding. Just don’t multiply more than once! If you do, the leavening and flavoring starts to get thrown off.

If you’re baking for a lot of people, Ina also suggests making recipes scaled for a baking sheet. As she explains, “It’s easier to bake, and it’s easier to cut.” We’d recommend Slab Chocolate Pecan Pie, Pumpkin Cake Bars with Cream Cheese Frosting, or Walnut and Oat Slab Pie.

While you may be excited to try out a show-stopping recipe from a brand-new cookbook, Ina recommends resisting temptation. “You don’t want to test something you haven’t made a million times,” she cautions. “Even for me, it adds enormous stress.” Instead of turning to a first-time recipe, Ina highly recommends that you “do the things you know will come out perfectly every time.” And if you do want to make a new-to-you recipe this Thanksgiving, consider baking it once or twice in the weeks leading up to the holiday, so you have the chance to practice and work out any kinks (like figuring out make-ahead components or fridge storage) before hosting on the big day.

No one wants a soggy bottom on their pie, especially a super-juicy one like apple pie. Erin McDowell has lots of tips to avoid a soggy bottom, but her biggest one is to precook your apple filling before assembling your pie. “We can't necessarily predict how juicy any given fruit is, so even if you're using the same apple … each time, they are going to be a little bit different,” she explained on our podcast episode all about apples. Precooking the filling allows you to adjust based on the juiciness of the fruit you’re using that day. “If you're dealing with not-very-juicy apples, you may not have to cook it as long. If they're really juicy, you may have to cook it longer, but the process is the same.”

Pie crust can be incredibly intimidating for some bakers. But pie queen Stacey Mei Yan Fong says the key to success is sometimes to chill out, figuratively and literally. “The best thing you can do is just chill, as in calm down. You're not doing brain surgery,” she reminded Jessica in our Thanksgiving pie episode. “And also use your fridge!” As she points out, “the minute things start getting too wet or things start getting too warm, that's when people panic.” So take a moment to chill the dough and make it easier to work with. If the butter's getting soft or your pie dough is getting difficult to work with, just put it in the fridge to cool down for 10 to 15 minutes.

It’s not just the fridge, either — your freezer can also be incredibly helpful. Stacey likes to freeze her pies before baking, which she says helps the crimping hold its shape as it bakes. And remember that, as Stacey points out, “Pie is kind of a lesson in patience.”

On our special Holiday Q+A Extravaganza dropping November 23, King Arthur’s Director of Baking Education Amber Eisler has a crucial tip for avoiding runny apple pies — make sure you see your pie filling actively bubbling through the vents in your pie crust. As she explains, “It means the filling has boiled and bubbled, so the starches in the filling are actually thickening, which is a super important part of [avoiding a soupy pie].”

There’s a lot of cooking and baking happening around Thanksgiving, which means your oven can get pretty crowded, pretty quickly. But be wary of overfilling it. Even though your oven may have up to three racks inside, Martin Philips cautions against using them all at the same time. “Don’t make the mistake of crowding the oven when it comes to rolls. Let bread have its moment,” he says. Bake your pies the day before to save oven space; a turkey, too will stay hot out of the oven for hours. When baking rolls, you want “top and bottom heat, use your convection setting, make sure that air is moving,” says Martin. “Guard the rolls, y’all!”

Cookbook author and professional psychologist Helen Goh believes in the power of baking to bring meaning and fulfillment to our lives. During this busy holiday season, full of messy kitchens, family squabbles, lengthy to-do lists, and lots of hosting, it can be easy to get overwhelmed. But baking can actually be a calming, comforting activity right now. “Some people say baking makes them nervous because you have to focus,” says Helen. But part of the therapeutic effect of baking comes from forcing us to focus on a single task at hand. As she points out, “that takes you away from all that white noise about what you did yesterday or what's going to happen tomorrow,” and instead allows you to live mindfully in the moment.

Happy Thanksgiving from all of us here at King Arthur. If you run into baking troubles or just need a little support in the kitchen, our Baker’s Hotline is here to help.

Cover photo by Patrick Marinello; food styling by Kaitlin Wayne.

World Children's Day 2026: 6 ways to help kids feel heard

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

The backpack hits the floor, and "How was your day?" gets a one-word answer: "Fine." Kids don’t always say what’s on their mind, but a little time and attention may help them open up.

World Children’s Day is a good moment for parents, caregivers, and teachers to ask kids how they’re feeling and listen without trying to fix anything. The day honors children’s rights, including the right to be heard.

Here’s a look at what the day is about, plus a few activities that can help kids feel understood, at home or in the classroom.

World Children's Day is a global day of action for children, run by UNICEF. In 2026, it falls on Friday, November 20. This year’s theme is “My day. My rights. My future.” It invites children to tell world leaders what kind of future they want.

The day celebrates children and the rights they’re born with. In many places, children “take over” high-profile roles in media, sports, and government for the day. Landmarks light up blue to show support for children's rights.

November 20 marks two milestones for children’s rights. On this date in 1959, the UN General Assembly adopted the Declaration of the Rights of the Child, a statement of shared ideals. Thirty years later to the day, it adopted the Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1989, a treaty that nations can commit to follow.

The Convention sets out the rights every child has, including the right to be safe, to play, and to have their opinions taken seriously. Nearly every country has formally agreed to follow it, and it’s the most widely ratified human rights treaty in history.

Countries around the world mark World Children’s Day. Many also celebrate a separate Children’s Day, most notably June 1, International Children's Day.

Some countries do pick dates of their own. Turkey celebrates on April 23, and India on November 14. The dates differ, but the idea is the same: it’s a day dedicated to the youngest members of a community.

You don’t need a big event to mark World Children’s Day. A meta-analysis on children and teens linked understanding emotions with better social skills and fewer behavior problems. Kids can build that understanding in everyday moments, like the ones below.

Kids may not know they have rights, but those who learn about them may feel more confident speaking up. UNICEF’s child-friendly version of the Convention explains the rights in plain language.

Try: Read one or two rights together, then ask which feels most important to them.

Not every child has the words for how they feel. Invite them to sketch their day from morning to night, then have them walk you through it.

If they’re up for it, ask which part they loved most and which part felt hard. A drawing gives kids something concrete to point to as they talk, and that can feel easier than answering a broad question such as “How are you feeling?”

Try: At home, pin the drawing to the fridge so your child can talk about it again if they’d like. In class, create an “Our Day” wall where kids can choose to share their drawings and spot the moments they had in common.

When a child melts down, get curious about the feeling underneath. Help them put it into words: “It looks like you’re frustrated that it’s not your turn. Is that right?” Then listen carefully to their answer before you reassure them. “You’re fine” can sound dismissive, even when you mean well. When you talk about feelings this way, kids often learn to do it too.

Try: Post a chart of faces showing common feelings where kids can see it, and invite them to point to or say how they feel at the start of the day. Welcome any details they want to share.

💙 Once a big feeling has a name, here are coping skills for kids to try.

Set aside a few minutes at dinner or morning circle time for everyone to share one high, one low, and one thing they’re thankful for. Take a turn yourself so kids feel more at ease sharing.

Just listen as kids share, and try not to talk them out of a hard feeling. Kids tend to manage feelings better when you respond with warmth instead of brushing them off.

Try: Pass around a talking object, such as a small stuffed animal. Whoever holds it has the floor, grown-ups included.

💙 On Calm, listen to Feel Thankful With Thomas, a four-minute meditation for little ones, or the Gratitude session from the Classroom Mindfulness Tools series.

A few minutes of meditation can be a soothing way for kids to slow down and settle. Sessions can focus on anything from breathing and body awareness to gratitude and kindness, so there’s room to follow your child’s interests or needs. Kids can sit, lie down, or stand, and they can stop whenever they’d like.

Try: Belly breathing is a gentle place to start. Have your child lie down with a stuffed animal on their belly and watch it rise and fall as they breathe slowly.

💙 Explore Calm’s Mindfulness for Kids sessions for ages 3–4, ages 5–6, ages 7–10, and ages 11–13. For little ones, try the Calm Your Worries With Percy meditation from the Thomas & Friends Learn to Meditate series.

Reading together gives you some time to connect, and stories can spark conversations about feelings. Kids may find it easier to talk about how a character feels than how they do.

Pick a book where a character is going through difficult feelings, then pause and ask, “What do you think she’s feeling?”

Try: At home, make the story the last part of your child’s bedtime routine to help them wind down. In class, read a few pages at the end of the day.

💙 Too tired to read at bedtime? Discover Calm’s library of kids' Sleep Stories, including A Tale of Forest Friends, Ella’s Treasure, and Close Your Eyes SleepyPaws.

World Children's Day is on Friday, November 20, 2026. It falls on the same date every year.

The UNICEF theme for World Children’s Day 2026 is "My day. My rights. My future." It encourages children to share their hopes for the future with world leaders.

The Convention on the Rights of the Child is the UN treaty behind the day. It names the rights every child has, including the right to be heard. For kids, it’s a written promise that their well-being and opinions count. Nearly every country has formally committed to it.

No, World Children’s Day and International Children’s Day are two separate observances with similar goals. World Children's Day is UNICEF's celebration on November 20, while International Children's Day is the June 1 observance held in many countries. Both honor children and their rights, so you can mark either one or both.

The best World Children’s Day activities for kids are often simple ones, such as inviting them to draw their day, holding a family or classroom check-in, or reading a story together. You can do these activities wherever makes sense, and even a few minutes counts.

💙 Looking for more quick ideas? Here are mindfulness activities for kids.

You can start with UNICEF’s child-friendly version of the Convention, and keep your explanation clear and specific. Try saying, “You have the right to feel safe, to play, and to say what you think.” Let them ask questions, and remind them that these rights belong to every child everywhere.

How Couples Create Their Own Unique Microcultures

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Do you have a pet name for your partner? I do, and many people I know do, too. Calling your loved one by a special nickname imbued with meaning—like “Bunny,” “Lumba,” or “Buncakes”—communicates in shorthand your unique connection and relational history.

Using pet names is just one example of what some researchers refer to as “microculture”—a combination of shared language, rituals, stories, histories, and more that evolve between small groups of people in close relationships and help sustain them. Maya Rossignac-Milon of Barcelona’s IESE Business School and her research collaborators are looking into how microculture could play a role in the very smallest of groups, romantic couples, and whether it holds clues to how these relationships thrive over time.

“People experience this commonly, and our initial research suggests that it’s super pervasive,” says Rossignac-Milon. “We are basically proposing that attraction and, more importantly, relationship satisfaction is something that’s created together in the moment.”

Much relationship research focuses on how individual traits (like extraversion, emotional expressiveness, or attachment style) affect relationship dynamics. But some of the results of that research suggests these factors may have less importance than assumed on relationship satisfaction and longevity.

Rossignac-Milon and her colleagues believe that the co-development of microculture may help explain those findings.

“Two people can be quite different from each other, but, with microculture, they’re building something together that didn’t exist before their relationship, that’s totally unique to the two of them,” says Rossignac-Milon. “And it’s that process of cocreation that can be really powerful.”

When people fall in love, they start merging their identities in certain ways and expanding their sense of self to include the other person. Creating a special microculture, consisting of inside jokes, pet names, repeated stories, and more, is one way couples cement their special connection.

What does this look like in practice? Rossignac-Milon says there are three core factors that move everyday interactions into the realm of microculture:

All three of these should be present for something to be part of your microculture. Consider this example. Every year, some friends of mine celebrate “the miracle of the tent,” a playful reference to the moment their platonic friendship turned unexpectedly romantic. Imbued with humor and meaning, this idiosyncratic holiday is part of their microculture, reinforcing their unique bond over time.

Shared origin stories are a common way that couples create microculture, helping to sustain their relationship. When they reminisce about turning points in their relationship and share their story with others, it can increase relationship satisfaction, research suggests. Couples who initiate nostalgic recollections of past shared experiences feel closer to their partners and are more committed. Perhaps that’s why my husband Don and I like to prompt people to share their origin story when meeting them for the first time. (Not to mention, they often ask us to return the favor—a win-win!)

Inside jokes can also express a couple’s microculture. For example, Don once bought me a t-shirt that he promised to wear with the words, “You’re right, dear,” printed on the front. I immediately got the joke—a humorous acknowledgment of times he’d insisted he was right about something when he wasn’t. Now it’s become a much-used catchphrase for admitting our mistakes. (He needs to say it more often than I do, of course).

Relationship rituals can also be part of your unique microculture if they are symbolically meaningful (and not just routine). Bringing coffee to your wife in bed on a silver tray might be your tongue-in-cheek way of acknowledging that she’s not a morning person and needs princess pampering. Or eating at the same Italian restaurant the first Friday of every month might be a nod to how you fell in love there over a plate of spaghetti. These small rituals communicate shared history and meaning, helping couples feel part of something special.

My colleague Katherine and her husband Brian developed a shorthand for recognizing moments when their dual-career family feels stretched thin. If either one needs to work late, instead of expressing frustration or being passive-aggressive, the other one says, “Thank you for doing that for our family.” This acknowledges that, while each of them would prefer to be relaxing with their kids and spouse, duty calls, and they’re doing the best they can to manage their work demands.

Shared language, stories, and rituals are often the most visible aspects of microculture, says Rossignac-Milon. But, just like macrocultures, microcultures also involve a shared sense of identity, morality, and reality.

Romantic partners tend to form identities as a couple that are distinct from their individual identities and serve to strengthen their relationship. For example, they may consider themselves to be “cat people” or a “power couple.” When partners are clear about their identity as a couple, it’s tied to higher levels of commitment.

Having a shared moral code (shared values and norms) is also part of a couple’s microculture. This might include rules like never working on Sundays, requiring both people to apologize after a conflict, or being sexually exclusive on weekends only. If broken, these norms may elicit anger, hurt, or the need to repair; so, the extent to which they are agreed upon and easily managed strengthens a couple’s well-being.

A shared sense of reality supports couples in their relationship, too. In a study run by Rossignac-Milon and her colleagues, they found that couples who perceive an overlap in how they view the world with their partner feel less uncertainty and more meaning in life than if they think their partner diverges from their worldview. When experiments manipulate a couple’s sense of agreement on their perceptions to signal disagreement, the researchers found, couples will try to compensate by leaning more heavily on shared microcultural references, as if to repair the rift in their shared reality.

This piece is part of a three-year GGSC project to explore love's role in promoting social well-being, bridging divides, and contributing to a good, meaningful life.

“People really care about this experience, and when it’s threatened, they want to protect their sense of, ‘No, we do have our own shared culture that we’ve developed together,’” says Rossignac-Milon.

While aspects of microculture within couples have mostly been described theoretically by drawing on past research, Rossignac-Milon and her colleagues have begun to research the overall concept more empirically, too.

In a currently unpublished study, she and her collaborators asked couples from diverse backgrounds to list three examples of each potential element of their microculture—from shared language to shared reality. Then, the couples kept daily diaries for two weeks, where they reported any references to their microculture that day and their feelings about their relationship. The researchers found that couples were happier with their relationship on days when their microculture was referenced more than usual. Also, looking at patterns across couples, those who referenced their microculture more were more satisfied with and committed to their relationship than those who referenced it less.

“People who tend to have more microculture and use it more are happier in their relationships and are more connected to and committed to their partners across the board,” says Rossignac-Milon.

Though microculture may help couples feel more connected and committed, there can be dark sides to the phenomenon, too, says Rossignac-Milon. Just like how some macrocultural norms and behaviors can make groups more insular or encourage shared false beliefs, microcultures can sometimes become problematic.

For example, sharing perceptions or beliefs with your partner may increase your certainty in your viewpoint and boost your convictions, perhaps in a way that isolates you from others, says Rossignac-Milon. Similarly, you can sometimes reinforce unhealthy behaviors, like encouraging each other to smoke if you identify as a couple that smokes together, health risks be damned.

A more extreme example might be how microculture can develop into a shared psychosis or support abusive relationships, says Rossignac-Milon.

“If the rituals and norms that people establish together are toxic to begin with or have power dynamics that are fostering inequality and an asymmetry in the relationship, that can be dangerous,” she says. This suggests that couples should pay attention to any darker aspects of microculture that could have these nefarious effects.

On the plus side, knowing the importance of microculture in romantic relationships can help people make dating more productive, says Rossignac-Milon. Instead of just looking for “Mr. or Ms. Right”—someone who checks off your boxes in terms of traits you want in a partner—pay attention to what’s created between you when spending time together. That playful banter or those surprising connections are part of what makes people feel like they click.

“Microculture is all about the experience of interacting and creating inside jokes and ways of using words that you have together and matter so much,” says Rossignac-Milon. “Letting yourself lean into that and using that as a diagnostic cue could be one important signal of compatibility that people are not always attending to.”

To encourage more of that special connection, she suggests taking small risks and not just saying what you think is expected of you. Those little idiosyncrasies or moments of vulnerability can serve to attract someone to you more than what’s on their resume.

“Put yourself out there and use a funny word or do something that’s a little bit different from what others might do,” she suggests. “Those are the things that can easily turn into inside jokes and microculture that you can reference back to.”

Couples in ongoing relationships can also benefit from paying attention to their microculture, she adds. It’s sometimes more natural to do that early on in a relationship and then neglect it over time. But continuing to reference your microculture and building on it is a way of keeping your relationship strong.

“The more that people can keep growing their microculture, the more they can continue to reap those relational benefits,” says Rossignac-Milon.

To nurture your microculture, it’s helpful to keep a spirit of joint creativity alive in your relationship and to introduce new experiences, inside jokes, rituals, and more. Otherwise, they can get old and stop having the same beneficial effect—similar to the way jokes can get stale and less funny over time, says Rossignac-Milon.

On the other hand, there’s no need to put pressure on yourself, either, she says. Building rapport through microculture is something that anyone can do, regardless of who you are.

“Jointly riffing, this kind of playful cocreation, may be something that some people are naturally good at, but it’s also a skill that can be developed by anyone, as long as they’re coming into it with spontaneity and a willingness to be a little bit vulnerable.”

Jill Suttie, Psy.D., is Greater Good’s former book review editor and now serves as a staff writer and contributing editor for the magazine. She received her doctorate of psychology from the University of San Francisco in 1998 and was a psychologist in private practice before coming to Greater Good.

« Il m’appelait tous les jours » : Thierry Henry déballe tout sur Michael Olise !

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Invité dans le podcast Kampo animé par le journaliste Smaïl Bouabdellah, Thierry Henry s'est longuement livré sur les coulisses des Jeux Olympiques de Paris 2024. L'ancien sélectionneur de l'équipe de France olympique a révélé la détermination totale de Michael Olise pour disputer le tournoi sous le maillot tricolore.

En 2024 la confection de la liste pour les Jeux Olympiques de Paris avait rapidement viré au casse-tête pour le sélectionneur tricolore. Le tournoi olympique de football n'étant pas inscrit sur les dates officielles du calendrier de la FIFA, les clubs professionnels n'avaient aucune obligation légale de libérer leurs joueurs. Cette absence de contrainte réglementaire avait grandement compliqué la tâche de Thierry Henry pour composer son groupe. Face à ces nombreux refus, Michael Olise a adopté une démarche diamétralement opposée.

L'attaquant a fait le forcing pour s'assurer une place dans le groupe olympique. Alors que Gareth Southgate souhaitait le convoquer avec les A de l'équipe d'Angleterre pour disputer l'Euro 2024, Michael Olise a écarté cette opportunité pour privilégier le maillot tricolore. Le joueur a ainsi multiplié les appels téléphoniques directs avec le sélectionneur français. Dans le podcast Kampo animé par Smaïl Bouabdellah, Thierry Henry a détaillé cette insistance quotidienne : « Michael, il m’appelait tous les jours pour aller aux JO, alors qu’il y avait l’équipe d’Angleterre qui le voulait pour aller au Championnat d’Europe avec les A. Il aurait pu aller à l’Euro avec l’Angleterre. Mais il m’appelait tous les jours. Son envie de jouer pour l’équipe de France était extraordinaire. Moi, je n’ai convaincu personne. »

Né à Londres en 2001 d'un père nigérian et d'une mère franco-algérienne, Michael Olise disposait sur le plan sportif de plusieurs options internationales. L'attaquant était éligible pour quatre sélections nationales différentes : la France, l'Angleterre, le Nigeria et l'Algérie. Toutefois, Michael Olise a toujours accordé sa préférence absolue à l'équipe de France. Au-delà des approches anglaises, le joueur a fait preuve d'une fermeté totale au moment de quitter Crystal Palace pour rejoindre l'Allemagne.

Recruté par le Bayern Munich pour 53 millions d'euros selon Foot Mercato, et plus de 60 millions d'euros d'après d'autres sources, il a imposé sa participation aux Jeux Olympiques de Paris 2024. Le joueur a ainsi pris le risque de se heurter à sa nouvelle direction bavaroise pour défendre les couleurs tricolores. Thierry Henry a salué cette prise de position au micro de Smaïl Bouabdellah : « Michael Olise aime l’équipe de France depuis qu’il est petit. Certains joueurs ne voulaient pas venir. Pas de problème, je n’en veux à personne. Mais un mec de ce calibre est venu, alors qu’il y avait le risque de s’embrouiller avec son nouveau club. Il m’a dit: 'Où je vais, je vais dire à mon club que je vais aux Jeux olympiques, c’est tout.’ On parle de Michael Olise. Mon respect pour lui est énorme. »

Sur le terrain, ce choix a porté ses fruits tout au long de la compétition estivale. Titulaire lors des 6 matches du tournoi olympique, Michael Olise a compilé 2 buts et 5 passes décisives sous les ordres de Thierry Henry. Ce parcours s'est achevé par une médaille d'argent décrochée le 9 août 2024 au Parc des Princes, après une finale disputée contre l'Espagne (3-5 après prolongation).

Au-delà de ce bilan chiffré, Thierry Henry qualifie Michael Olise de génie qu'il ne faut pas brider. Dans le podcast Kampo, l'ancien joueur d'Arsenal a ainsi développé son analyse sur le talent de son ancien joueur : « Michael, il est à part parce qu’il pense différemment. À un moment donné, les génies, il ne faut pas essayer de les comprendre. C’est un génie ? Ah, de très loin, très très loin. (…) Est-ce que tu vas, toi, dire à un peintre comment peindre ? Pourquoi tu veux bloquer un génie ? ».

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

I suspect that quite a few older investors are in a similar situation, concerned only with the income produced. Comments and corrections welcome.
Not in tax brackets where munis make sense, so I do this with junk bonds. The higher yield ties up less capital in bonds for a given level of income, so expected returns are higher overall as more stocks can be held. It's loading beta, loosely speaking, rather than the usual minimum volatility design around bonds. But if the resulting income's sufficient to cover expenses then the increase in volatility's not a major functional concern, just requires the investor risk tolerance is there. It also happens that, personally, I prefer tilting towards credit risk rather than duration risk.

Some examples here are multisector (VMSAX BBB- 3.8y, VGMS BB+ 3.8y), active junk (VWEAX BB- 3.1y, VGHY BB- 3.3y, VEGBX BB+ 6.1y), junk index (VCHY B+ 3.3y, SPHY B+ 3.2y), and mezzanine CLOs (CLOZ BB+ 0.2y). Mezzanine offers some modest cyclical diversification, higher credit ratings (JBBB BBB 0.1y). historically lower default impacts, and lower portfolio effective durations. Active and index junk used to sit around 2.7y but has been lengthening lately. If we're currently in a bond secular bear and it goes like the last one those appear to me to be plausibly useful properties.

I regularly look at short-term investment grade (VCSH A- 2.7y, VTES AA 2.7y, VGSH AA 1.9y) and question whether the credit risk premium's worth it, to which I'd say short corporate's gotten pretty competitive lately (also VUSB A 0.9y). So I'm curious about the upcoming part 2. I also look at US intermediates (VCIT A-, 5.9y, VWALX 8.6y, VWIUX AA- 6.0y, VCIT AA 4.8y) for the duration premium and I haven't felt it to be compelling since ZIRP1.
What factor causes the longer duration not to compensate?
I've been inclined to view it as the market failing to adequately price in volatility drag, though perhaps a rigorous look would find otherwise. For example, there might be enough laddering that's not really how the price setting works.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: Mike Piper (Boglehead) new book out [Household Cybersecurity]

📅 2026-11-12  |  Read Source →

Currently, my wife and I have a shared Google doc that lists our passwords. We'll migrate to a password manager as part of our plan. But if the Google doc sits behind our individual Google access passwords, isn't the Google doc similarly protected as the information in Google Password Manager would be?
The two options have certain points in common in that in both cases, the data is encrypted in transit, encrypted on Google's servers, Google stores the keys to decrypt, and it would be the same login credentials that provide access.

There are a variety of other differences though.

Google Password Manager offers the option for on-device encryption, if desired:
https://support.google.com/accounts/answer/11350823

Google Password Manager would provide some degree of phishing protection in that the auto-fill feature won't work if you're on the wrong domain.

Google Password Manager will flag weak/reused passwords for you and notify you if any of your saved passwords are exposed in a known breach.

It might be easier to accidentally share the doc (e.g., you or your spouse accidentally setting it to "anybody with the link"). Or perhaps via a third-party app being given access to read your documents.

Also, I don't use Google Password Manager, so I'm not sure about this one. But the "somebody grabbed my laptop and ran off with it, while I was signed in" scenario might be less bad with Google Password Manager, depending on your settings. For example you may be able to set it so that you have to re-authenticate (e.g., by fingerprint) before it will let you use a stored password.
I am testing Google Password Manager. With my current configuration, someone with my unlocked computer can't go to the password manager and see any passwords without re-authenticating. But if they go to a website and start the login process, then most websites will let them see the password by clicking the "show" button. I don't know if there is any way to prevent that,

if annual spending is a lavish 250k (could be hard to spend?).
